===PAGE 3=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                              IN MEMORIAM
   2|                                                           Paul Joseph Doherty, 64, May 14, 2019
   3|                                                            Paul was a fixture in the tri-city area as he currently served on the Lunenburg Zoning
   4|                                                            Board and the Leominster Housing Board. Paul had the self-proclaimed "gift of gab"
   5|                                                            and would strike up a conversation with anyone in ear shot and quickly endeared
   6|                                                            himself to complete strangers. Paul was a very kind and considerate soul who would
   7|                                                           take the time to help anyone in need and always find a solution to a problem no
   8|                                                            matter how complex. Paul will truly be missed by all that loved him and all that he
   9|                                                           loved and will be forever in his family's hearts and minds.
  10|                                                     Helen D. "Denise" Galloway, 65, June 10, 2019
  11|                                                     For over 23 years, Denise worked for the Lunenburg Public Schools. She worked in the
  12|                                                     library at Turkey Hill and was the administrative secretary at the former T.C. Passios and
  13|                                                     Lunenburg Primary. To many, Denise was the first person parents spoke with when
  14|                                                     enrolling their children in school. She will be remembered for her kind heart and the way
  15|                                                     she could reassure even the most nervous new students. She greeted everyone with a
  16|                                                     warm and welcoming smile. To all who knew and loved Denise, she will be deeply
  17|                                                     missed.
  18|                                                      Catherine "Catie" Keefer, 40 June 14, 2019
  19|                                                     Catie along with her sister owned and operated a daycare in Lunenburg for several years.
  20|                                                     She was a lifeguard and swim instructor at the Lunenburg Town Beach in the summer
  21|                                                     months. She was also very active in Lunenburg Youth Soccer for many years as a coach
  22|                                                     and a parent. Some of her favorite things were going to the ocean whenever possible, her
  23|                                                    lime green Jeep Wrangler, annual trips to Disney World and Gettysburg, PA. The most
  24|                                                     important people to her were her two sons, and her family.
  25|                                                     Janice P. (Davies) Carrier ,75, September 9, 2019
  26|                                                    Jan worked as the Administrative Assistant for the Conservation Commission. She retired
  27|                                                     from the position that she loved in December 2015. Jan loved her community and
  28|                                                     demonstrated that through her service to the same. Whether serving on building
  29|                                                     committees, the Cemetery Commission, the Historical Society, the Caucus Committee or
  30|                                                    any of several other groups, she always had something to give. She was a dedicated
  31|                                                    member and former secretary of the Lunenburg Turkey Hills Family Lions Club. She
  32|                                                     served as Lunenburg's Election Warden for over 45 years. and was usually the first face
  33|                                                     you would see in the lobby upon entering to cast your ballot. Jan enjoyed spending time
  34|                                                     out and about with friends and family. Her children and grandchildren meant the world to
  35|                                                     her.
  36|                                                             Patrick Michael Barney, 41, October 5, 2019
  37|                                                             Patrick was appointed as a Reserve Officer at the Lunenburg Police Department
  38|                                                             July 1, 2000 and became a full-time Officer on January 13, 2002. He was a Squad
  39|                                                             Leader when he graduated from the Massachusetts Police Corps. He always hoped
  40|                                                             that Lunenburg would initiate a canine force as he loved animals, especially his
  41|                                                             dachshund, Sable. After a critical incident in 2006, Pat developed PTSD and as a
  42|                                                             result retired from the Police Department on August 8, 2012. Patrick then lived
  43|                                                             between Massachusetts and Florida. Patrick had a joyous smile, infectious laugh and
  44|                                                             was always willing to help others. Even in his death, he donated his organs so that
  45|                                                            others could enjoy life

===PAGE 9=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                                                                          LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                                                       YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728
   3|                                                                        REGISTERED VOTERS-7870
   4|                                                                            FY' 19 TAX RATE-$18.68
   5|                           OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   6|                                                    ANNUAL TOWN MEETING-IST SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                                                   ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3R SATURDAY IN MAY
   8|                                                                        AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
   9|                                                                          ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
  10|                          Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of
  11|                          approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
  12|                          approximately 43 miles from Boston. It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and
  13|                          the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on
  14|                          the east by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
  15|                          Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered
  16|                          voters in the town. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of
  17|                          the town are generally administered by a five member Board of Selectmen and a Town
  18|                          Manager. Local school affairs are administered by an elected seven member School
  19|                          Committee while local taxes are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all are
  20|                          appointed on an at-large basis for staggered three-year terms.
  21|                          Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water
  22|                          bodies account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development.
  23|                          Lake Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District
  24|                          from the remainder of Lunenburg. This district was developed as a housing base for many
  25|                          factory workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg. In many
  26|                          ways the characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these
  27|                          cities than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area of town, around Hickory Hills
  28|                          Lake, was developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The
  29|                          third body of water is Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly
  30|                           vacation cottages that have now been modified to year-round residences. Commercial and
  31|                          industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major
  32|                          arteries (Routes 2A and 13), and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of
  33|                           Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards
  34|                          had slowly developed into single family housing, tarming continues on in Lunenburg.

   1|                                                                ADMINISTRATION
   2|                                                                                               19

===PAGE 23=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                           REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                        Each year that I sit down to write this report I pour over the minutes of the previous year to
   3|                        recollect all of the achievements we were able to complete. Some may think the wheels of
   4|                        government turn slowly, but for those of us that have chosen local government as our careers
   5|                        and know the various steps and stakeholders involved to get things done, it is amazing all that
   6|                        we are able to accomplish. The calendar year of 2019 was no exception. The Town Manager
   7|                        and Board of Selectmen continue to work hard on serving the best interests of the Town and
   8|                        providing a high level of services while remaining fiscally responsible to the taxpayers.
   9|                        During 2019 there were many topics addressed and accomplishments to highlight:
  10|                        • As part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed their goals from FY19
  11|                              and formalized their goals for FY20. The goals the Board identified for FY2019 included
  12|                             identifying, defining, discussing, and finalizing the criteria to be used in determining the
  13|                             use and/or re-use of the Town Hall, Ritter, old Primary, Brooks House, and TC Passios and
  14|                             to propose at least one project to the 2020 Annual Town Meeting regarding the use and/or
  15|                             reuse of the old Primary School. The Board identified the criteria used in determining the
  16|                              use and/or re-use of the aforementioned buildings in the fall of 2019 and discussed the
  17|                             future use of the old Primary throughout the year and continued to narrow down the focus
  18|                              for the future use of this building into 2020.
  19|                        • The Town Manager's goals included developing and implementing steps towards the
  20|                             redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, implementation of a recycling
  21|                              contamination enforcement program, and planning/implementing accessibility
  22|                              improvements.
  23|                        • There were multiple projects that addressed accessibility at various town facilities, town
  24|                              property, and public rights of way throughout the year. In January the Town Manager
  25|                             announced receipt of an ADA Project Grant from MA Office on Disability for $7,500 for
  26|                              accessibility improvements to the Lane, Cowdrey, and Small Town Forest properties. Also
  27|                              in January the Veterans Service Office was relocated from the Eagle House to the Ritter
  28|                              Memorial Building to be more accessible for veterans as the previous office was located on
  29|                              a second floor without a handicap lift so the Veterans Officer was relocated to the Ritter
  30|                              Memorial Building lower level that has a lift. The Town Manager coordinated the use of
  31|                              Montachusett Technical students to renovate the second floor Town Hall restroom to
  32|                              become a handicapped accessible bathroom and this was started in February and completed
  33|                              by May. In March the Town Manager applied for and ultimately received a District Local
  34|                              Technical Assistance grant to update the Town's ADA Self-Evaluation and Transition
  35|                              Plan. The Town was one of three communities that participated in a Public Rights of Way
  36|                              study through our regional planning agency that used a consultant to review the town's
  37|                              sidewalks, ramps, and other ADA requirements. Around June the handicapped parking
  38|                              spot at Town Hall was moved from an uneven brick sidewalk to a concrete level spot, the
  39|                              chairlift door at Town Hall was fixed, and the uneven granite steps at the Ritter Memorial
  40|                              Building were repaired.
  41|                                                                                                21

===PAGE 24=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                          • In June the Town Manager applied for and received the Recycling IQ Kit Grant, which
   2|                               provided funding for educational materials and funds to hire Recycling Inspectors to do
   3|                               curbside checks of recycling bins in order to reduce the town's contamination rate. The
   4|                               nine week program targeted the areas in town with higher contamination rates and focused
   5|                               on educating the public on what materials can and cannot be recycled. At the conclusion
   6|                               of the program, the town's contamination rate was cut by twenty percent.
   7|                          • The redevelopment and/or reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, an old vacant gas station
   8|                               with potential environmental hazards, has been a property the Town has sought to clean-
   9|                               up for over a decade but due to the unknown environmental hazards have not taken the
  10|                               property for back taxes so that the onus of the potential clean-up costs was not borne by
  11|                               the Town. After much research and consultation with the State and legal counsel, we were
  12|                               able to gain an Administrative Search Warrant that gave the Town the ability to apply for
  13|                               grant funding to perform an environmental assessment and right to access the property for
  14|                               the inspection and sampling needed. In September the Court granted the Administrative
  15|                               Search Warrant and the Town Manager submitted a Brownfields Site Assessment Grant
  16|                               through Montachusett Regional Planning Agency for the costs of the environmental site
  17|                               assessment, which was approved. The environmental site assessment is expected to be
  18|                               complete by the summer of 2020 and the project will be eligible for clean-up costs
  19|                               through MassDevelopment.
  20|                         • In the fall of 2019 the Town Manager worked with a consultant to apply for grant funding
  21|                               through the Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Program, which provides
  22|                               support for cities and towns to plan for climate change and implement key adaptation
  23|                               actions for resiliency. The planning grant applied for is a necessary step in order to apply
  24|                               for construction costs to implement projects that are identified during the planning phase.
  25|                               In 2020, the Town was notified we received this grant.
  26|                         • After successfully applying for District Local Technical Assistance from our regional
  27|                               planning agency to create a business brochure and create a permitting guidebook, both
  28|                               were completed in January. The Business Brochure was created with assistance from the
  29|                               Lunenburg Business Association and Land Use Director in an effort to highlight the
  30|                               town's natural resources and increase awareness of these locations to residents who many
  31|                               not know where public access to trails and conservation lands are located. The Permitting
  32|                               Guidebooks was created to assist customers with projects of any type and scale to simplify
  33|                               how they can file permits. Another project that was applied for and received through our
  34|                              regional planning agency was for a Walkability and Parking Study. This study focused on
  35|                               traffic data, crash data, an inventory of bike and pedestrian infrastructure for the Main
  36|                               Street area and Whalom Lakefront area. The report was finalized in November and
  37|                              identified a number of recommendations to address improving the walkability and parking
  38|                              in both the Main Street and Whalom area.
  39|                                                                                              22

===PAGE 25=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                        • The Town Manager, DPW Director, and engineering firm WorldTech presented a
   2|                              proposal to the Selectmen for a Tier 3 Complete Streets application that the Board agreed
   3|                              to submit to MassDOT for Tier 3 funding totaling $258,000 in September of 2019. This
   4|                              application, which included improvements to the Main Street area, Memorial Drive, and
   5|                              the Main Street/Oak Avenue intersection. The Town received notification it was awarded
   6|                              $258,000 in January of 2020.
   7|                        • The future of town facilities continued to be a theme in 2019. Each month a workshop
   8|                              was held with different stakeholders that would be affected by decisions relative to the
   9|                              future of the TC Passios, old Primary Building, Ritter Memorial, Town Hall, and the
  10|                               Brooks House. On January 15 the Selectmen had a joint meeting with the Architectural
  11|                              Preservation District Commission, Historical Commission, and Historical Society to
  12|                               discuss their concerns regarding the condition of the old Primary School and future of the
  13|                              building. At the conclusion of the meeting, Chairman Jamie Toale stated the goal of the
  14|                              Board was to ask town meeting for funding for detailed plans for the TC Passios so that an
  15|                              actual cost to move town offices there would be known. In February the Board met with
  16|                              the Conservation Commission, Open Space, and the Parks Commission to discuss the plan
  17|                              to use TC Passios for municipal office space, school central offices, and community
  18|                              space. In March the Board met with the Public Access Cable Committee and town
  19|                              employees to hear their input on consolidating town offices at the TC Passios building. In
  20|                               May the Board met with another group of stakeholders including the Council on Aging
  21|                              members, COA Director, Library Trustees, Library Director, and Teen Center personnel.
  22|                              In September the Board met with the Architectural Preservation District Committee to
  23|                               discuss the old Primary building and the Brooks House, in terms of future use and how
  24|                               this aligned with the regulations under the APDC Bylaw. In November the Board
  25|                               conducted a survey of potential uses for the old Primary School that was handed out at the
  26|                               Special Town Meeting and the town meeting also included a non-binding vote on the
  27|                               demolition of the old Primary School. The Board continues to discuss this goal and move
  28|                               forward on a potential re-use.
  29|                         • On January 22°d our legislators, Senator Tran, Representative Benson and Representative
  30|                               Hay came before the board to announce the $130,000 state earmark for abatement of
  31|                               asbestos at the Turkey Hill Elementary School.
  32|                         • In March the Town partnered with the solar company Nexamp to promote their
  33|                               community solar program to Lunenburg residents for discounted electricity. The program
  34|                               was so successful it reached its cap and had a waiting list. Later in the year, Nexamp
  35|                               secured another solar location that enabled those on the waiting list to take advantage of
  36|                               these discounts.
  37|                         • In March the Selectmen created the Land Acquisition Committee to examine and
  38|                               negotiate the purchase of land to be used for conservation and recreation purposes at 994
  39|                               Northfield Road. Ultimately, the Committee was successful in negotiating a price of
  40|                               $775,000, which was below the appraised value and secured $500,000 in grant funds
  41|                               towards the purchase of land to be used for conservation and recreations purposes at 994
  42|                               Northfield Road. Town meeting approved the purchase of land at the November 2019
  43|                               Special Town Meeting.
  44|                                                                                               23

===PAGE 26=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                        • The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2020 to the Finance
   2|                              Committee and Selectmen in mid-February. Part of the Town Manager's recommendation
   3|                              was to continue to fund the Other Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded
   4|                              liability of retiree health insurance in the amount of $150,000, continue funding the Special
   5|                              Purpose Stabilization Account for known large capital items in the amount of $200,000, and
   6|                              fund the regular Stabilization fund in the amount of $100,000. The FY2020 budget also
   7|                              included the hiring of an additional Full-Time Patrolman, addressed the need for increased
   8|                              Overtime costs for the Fire Department, a part-time Recreation Director, and Assistant
   9|                             Town Manager/HR Director. The Annual Town Meeting was held on Saturday, May 4** and
  10|                              approved a total budget for FY20 of $38,058,164.30.
  11|                        • This year was another incredible year for road projects in Lunenburg. Besides the
  12|                             continuation of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town was the
  13|                              recipient of a Small Bridge Grant that was originally applied for in June of 2017 in the
  14|                             amount of $379,000 to reconstruct the Pleasant Street Bridge over Pearl Hill Brook. The
  15|                              project requires design work phases that must be approved by MassDOT as part of the
  16|                             process. The project is expected to be complete by the spring of 2022. The DP W also
  17|                             undertook culvert repairs on Northfield Road and Sunset Lane and repairs to the Townsend
  18|                              Harbor Bridge. The Summer Street Road Reconstruction project that was awarded in 2018
  19|                              was substantially complete by the fall of 2019.
  20|                       •  The Town had begun the process of becoming a Dementia Friendly community in the latter
  21|                             half of 2018 and had a kick-off event in June of 2019. The Dementia Friendly Action group
  22|                             had a lot of success in a short amount of time and the initiatives they undertook were
  23|                             modeled by other communities.
  24|                        • As part of the Town Manager's Goals for FY19, the Town Manager completed an
  25|                             Employee Handbook and Procurement Manual. The Employee Handbook codified the
  26|                             town's hiring process, personnel policies and practices and the Procurement Manual
  27|                             provided written procedures for town departments for proper procurement of supplies,
  28|                             services, and construction projects. The Board adopted a Reimbursement Policy.
  29|                             The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY
  30|                             2020.
  31|                       • In July, as part of an annual review of the Selectmen's Policies and Procedures, the Town
  32|                              Manager presented various updates to the Board's Manual including a revised Recycling
  33|                             Policy, Public Comment Policy, Chapter 61 Policy, among other minor revisions to other
  34|                             policies. The Board also adopted a new Social Media policy.
  35|                       • In October the Town Manager, the School Superintendent, and the Land Use Director held a
  36|                             Symposium to discuss the historical growth patterns and projected future growth/
  37|                             development and how this would impact Town and School services and needs. Some of the
  38|                             patterns that were evident from the data was there would be a steady growth in the gaining
  39|                             population in Lunenburg, a rise in racial and ethnic diversity, the need for increased staffing
  40|                             and the need for additional space for added staff.
  41|                                                                                              24

===PAGE 27=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                         • The Town Manager presented a finalized Police Union agreement to the Board for a three
   2|                               year agreement in the fall that equated to a zero dollar impact to Fiscal Year 2020, mainly
   3|                               due to attrition in the Police Department during Fiscal Year 2020. The agreement
   4|                               included a 2.5% COLA for year one, 2% for year two, and 2% for year three and
   5|                               increased the educational incentive over three years. The costs for year two were
   6|                               estimated at $81,000 and year three costs were estimated at $74,000, with the total cost of
   7|                               the agreement to $139,000.
   8|                         • The Special Town Meeting in November included a number of articles such as amending
   9|                               the FY 2019 budget to capture additional tax growth, transferred available funds into the
  10|                               Special Purpose Stabilization account, the acquisition of property on Northfield Road for
  11|                               conservation and recreation purposes, by-laws changes, and two Home Rule petitions for
  12|                               additional liquor licenses.
  13|                         • In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler,
  14|                               weekday entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.
  15|                               Throughout the year, the Board reviewed Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers,
  16|                               Special Event permit requests, flammable storage licenses, pole petitions, tollbooth
  17|                               requests, and held Dangerous Dog hearings.
  18|                          As of writing this report, it has been three and a half years since I was appointed your Town
  19|                          Manager and it is a responsibility that I take very seriously. The residents of Lunenburg
  20|                          demonstrate the true meaning of community that I have witnessed in various acts, whether it
  21|                          be supporting our local businesses, non-profit groups raising funds for those in need, or
  22|                          words of gratitude for our public safety and public works departments. I have the privilege
  23|                          of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents and
  24|                          business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the
  25|                          public. The accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities,
  26|                          could not happen without the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials
  27|                          that make the wheels of town government turn. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine
  28|                          Peterson and Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for all your hard work and keeping the
  29|                          office running smoothly. I would also like to thank the committee and board members,
  30|                          department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  31|                          Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the department heads that lead our various
  32|                          departments: James Marino, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz,
  33|                          DW Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari,
  34|                          Treasurer/Collector; Louise Paquette, Assessors; Steve Malandrinos, IT Director; Adam
  35|                          Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA Director; Muir Haman, Library Director;
  36|                          Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk; and all their staff for their talent and working together to make
  37|                          our town run as efficiently as possible. Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses that
  38|                          make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  39|                          Respectfully submitted,
  40|                          Heather R. Lemieux
  41|                          Town Manager
  42|                                                                                               25

===PAGE 60=== (ocr, 32 lines)
   1|                                                                         BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   2|                         The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building
   3|                         located at 960 Massachusetts Avenue. The Building Department consists of the following
   4|                         personnel. Casey Burlingame serves as the Building Commissioner as well as the Zoning
   5|                         Officer. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Building Department,
   6|                         as well as the Zoning Board of Appeals Administrative Assistant. Jack Biery serves as the
   7|                         Wiring Inspector. Gary Williams serves as the Plumbing Inspector and Gas Inspector. The
   8|                         purpose of the Building Department is to ensure State regulations and Codes are being met as
   9|                         well as local bylaws. This includes new construction, alterations, additions, remodels and
  10|                         annual inspections of certain public use groups such as restaurants, churches, bars, daycares,
  11|                          meeting halls, schools, and others.
  12|                         Building Permits for fiscal year 2019 are as follows:
  13|                         120 gas permits
  14|                         122 plumbing permits
  15|                         248 electrical permits
  16|                         461 building permits, including driveway, mechanical, and fire suppression permits
  17|                         951 total permits issued
  18|                         The state of Massachusetts continues to follow a group of International Codes amended by
  19|                         each state. These codes cover a variety of building elements from mechanical systems, energy
  20|                        conservation, primary building practices, installation of fire suppression systems, and others.
  21|                         Currently we are in the 9th edition of 780 CMR, the Massachusetts State Building Code.
  22|                        In addition to the Building Codes and Zoning Bylaws, the Building Department also assists
  23|                        the Town Clerk with Business Certificates. This is achieved by way of a "Form of Intent".
  24|                        This form is used to ensure local bylaws are met by the applicant. Once the form is completed
  25|                        by the Building Department, a Business Certificate can be applied for at the Town Clerk's
  26|                        office.
  27|                         All applications for new work requiring permits are available online through the Town
  28|                        website. The Building Department continues to use ViewPoint online permitting. Paper
  29|                        applications are available at the Building Department office as well.
  30|                        Casey J. Burlingame
  31|                        Building Commissioner/Zoning Officer
  32|                                                                                              58

===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                                                                   CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   2|                        The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven-member commission, appointed to 3-year terms by
   3|                        the Board of Selectmen, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL chapter 40 $
   4|                        8C. The Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL chapter 131 § 40 and Chapter 239
   5|                        of the local bylaws for the protection of wetlands.
   6|                        The year 2019 was as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and
   7|                        meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep
   8|                        projects and larger scale industrial/commercial proposals along the Electric Avenue section of Lunenburg. The
   9|                        Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of preparing to use the year 2020 to work on clarifying the
  10|                        land use policy to provide consistency with the original bylaw and to find those sections that may be now out of
  11|                        date and need to be modified. This effort is estimated to be completed by summer of 2020. The Conservation
  12|                        Commission completed revisions to the local wetland regulations which are now in effect and were presented to
  13|                        the Town Clerk for posting.
  14|                        The Commission also held numerous inspections with the Commission Administrator on weekends prior to
  15|                       meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The
  16|                        Commission continues to work with the Town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates
  17|                        from the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification as well as
  18|                       aiding with the community Notice of Intent to the EPA under the "MS4" regulations.
  19|                        The Conservation Commission implemented its forest stewardship planning after receiving grants continuing the
  20|                        efforts outlined in last year's annual report. These stewardship plans, adding the Hollis Road property, is oriented
  21|                       for enhancing bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the DCR "Forestry
  22|                        for the Birds" and will aid the Commission in implementing environmental management plans which include
  23|                        timber stand management. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup of New England Forestry Consultants, a
  24|                        noted forester with many years of experience working in Lunenburg and all of the adjacent towns/cities in this
  25|                        region serving those communities and private landowners. The Conservation Commission also held public
  26|                        recreational walks through its open space parcels and plans to continue to do so. The Commission has, and
  27|                        continues to hold public forums on the implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management.
  28|                        As a result of some of the meetings and forums held, the Commission continues to apply for and receive funding
  29|                        from the Commonwealth to author stewardship plans for our Conservation Areas.
  30|                        Copies of the current environmental stewardship plans are available on the Conservation Commission website at:
  31|                        http://www.lunenburgma.gov/content/204/222/default.aspx
  32|                        This past year, the Commission successfully completed the collection of a single $11,000 fine for wetland
  33|                        violations on one single property. Aside from the collection of the fine, the Commission ordered and oversaw the
  34|                        successful remediation of the wetland resource areas being restored to pre-alteration condition.
  35|                       The Commission has also completed the successful implementation of the installation of handicap parking and
  36|                        handicap accessible trails at the Small Town Forest, Cowdrey and Lane Properties. The funding was provided by
  37|                        a grant from the Mass Office on Disability.
  38|                        The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills in the implementation
  39|                        of their management plans becoming actively involved in continuation of formulating updates designed to give a
  40|                        better picture of the overall health of the ecosystems and the various ways to ensure continued protection of these
  41|                        The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!
  42|                        Respectfully Submitted,
  43|                        Richard Bursch, Chair
  44|                        Todd Dwyer, Vice-Chair
  45|                        Bob Pease, Carl Luck, Jack Rabbitt, Kenneth Jones, Catie Childs
  46|                        Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator
  47|                                                                                               61

===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                                              COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|                                                                                             FY19
   3|                        The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   4|                        supportive services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts General
   5|                        Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or services and to
   6|                        develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   7|                        The FY 19 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative Assistant
   8|                        and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal Site Coordinator; Judy Alario and
   9|                        Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, Paul Grunditz, and James Barrett, MART Van Drivers.
  10|                        The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Board of
  11|                        Selectmen. The FY19 Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson
  12|                        Jacquelyn Dwyer, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally, Cheryl Moisan,
  13|                        Diane Nowd, Jane Rabbitt, and Judith Tarbell.
  14|                        The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
  15|                        Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and
  16|                        Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
  17|                        We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House. In FY 19 we had 57 volunteers who
  18|                        contributed 2,452.75 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the meals on wheels
  19|                        program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, and friendly visitors. We greatly
  20|                        appreciate their service.
  21|                        The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts, intergenerational,
  22|                        bocce, shuffle board, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, Memory Café and social events both at
  23|                        the senior center and offsite.
  24|                        There were approximately 978 people who utilized the senior center in FY19. Those 978 people compiled 28,764
  25|                       units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
  26|                       In FY 19 the Council on Aging delivered 4,319 Meals on Wheels to 46 clients and provided 2,964 rides to 71
  27|                        clients on our senior van.
  28|                        We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
  29|                        supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
  30|                        Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. This group provides funding for
  31|                       new living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, garden upkeep and spring
  32|                       mulch at the Eagle House Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for
  33|                        donating time and programming to our clients.
  34|                        The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
  35|                       www.ourseniorcenter.com or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town Hall,
  36|                       Library, Post Office, local churches and various businesses throughout the community.
  37|                                                                                              62

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                                  DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|                                        The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Parks, Cemetery,
   3|                            Sewer, and Facilities divisions and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing
   4|                            and scheduling personnel and equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the
   5|                            effective delivery of an extensive scope of services with a high degree of efficiency.
   6|                             "The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the Town's
   7|                            infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and exceeds the current and future social
   8|                            and economic needs of the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for
   9|                            the Town's residents and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and environmentally sound means
  10|                            of disposing of solid waste and wastewater."
  11|                            DEPARTMENT PHILOSOPHY
  12|                                        Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything
  13|                            worthwhile can be accomplished.
  14|                                        Efficiency: Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may take a bit longer but
  15|                            will not require repeated attempts.
  16|                                        Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.
  17|                                        Every employee is essential. Those at the top are support people. The real work is done by
  18|                            the men and women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide a
  19|                            seamless flow of paperwork.
  20|                            DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
  21|                                         Public Works staff is either working or on call twenty-four hours a day, three hundred and
  22|                             sixty-five days a year, prepared to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility
  23|                            related emergency that may arise.
  24|                                        We strive to enhance the awareness of the value Public Works provides to our Town, and
  25|                             ensure others are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to
  26|                            make sure efforts are not dismissed as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of
  27|                            service.
  28|                                        Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have
  29|                             highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency
  30|                            management as first responders. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is
  31|                             critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must continue to build on this
  32|                             awareness.
  33|                                        This department must reach out to the public we serve, continuing to make them aware of
  34|                             the fact that the common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a
  35|                             dedicated staff of individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control,
  36|                             solid waste collection and recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management, grounds
  37|                             maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are necessary services supporting
  38|                             everyone in the community.
  39|                                         We will continue to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather
  40|                             support for annual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  41|                                                                                               63

===PAGE 66=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                         DEPARTMENTAL GOALS
   2|                         • Continue to coordinate administrative directives with the activities of all public service departments
   3|                               and organizations within the DPW to improve service levels. Strive always for a higher level of
   4|                              efficiency.
   5|                         • Coordinate with those who work daily in Town facilities to best prioritize capital improvements.
   6|                               DPW plays a major role in the enhancement of the work environment of all Town employees.
   7|                         • Increase involvement with our insurance provider to make better use of available resources in an
   8|                               effort to obtain credits toward lessening premiums. Safety is our first concern.
   9|                         • Set realistic expectations. Make clear through public education that due to limited resources,
  10|                               particularly in the area of personnel, the level of service provided by Public Works is not always
  11|                              optimal.
  12|                         • Work diligently to establish and maintain a high level of courteous communication and interaction
  13|                              between members of all boards and commission.
  14|                         • Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and
  15|                               value a plan for rehabilitation
  16|                        PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  17|                                     It had become a high priority goal of the Board of Selectmen as Road Commissioners, to
  18|                        achieve a pavement condition of 72%-75% town-wide. While state-supported Chapter 90 funds remain
  19|                         stagnant, the dedicated Pavement Management line in the operational budget has grown substantially.
  20|                         Additionally, with the infusion of funds facilitated by a four million dollar debt exclusion supported by
  21|                         residents, the Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan has been hugely successful providing
  22|                         smooth and safe transportation for our residents and all those travelling through our community.
  23|                                    The debt exclusion funding combined with state and operational funds has nearly tripled the
  24|                        total paving value of years past as we attack a nine million dollar backlog of repairs.
  25|                                    Time spent addressing potholes and other roadway deficiencies diminish exponentially as new
  26|                         paving increases. A clear benefit of the dollars expended. There is much work yet to do but expectations
  27|                                    The Summer Street project burst into full development after many years of aggressive lobbying
  28|                        by this department, Town officials and members of our legislative branch. As of this writing, the ten
  29|                        million dollar rehabilitation of sub-surface utilities and total reconstruction of the roadway from
  30|                        Leominster to John Fitch Highway in Fitchburg is complete. This represents the single largest roadway
  31|                         project in the history of the Town of Lunenburg.
  32|                                    In addition, funding has been procured for the reconstruction of the Pleasant St. Bridge through
  33|                         the State's Small Bridge program. We have also been awarded a grant to enhance the "walkability" in
  34|                        town center by way of a successful application to the Complete Streets Design Committee and the
  35|                         Office of the Secretary of Transportation.
  36|                                     Acquiring supplemental funding allows the completion of projects that would otherwise not be
  37|                         viable due to the extraordinary expense.
  38|                        FLEET MAINTENANCE
  39|                        The DPW Maintenance Division is responsible for the repair and preventative maintenance to all Town
  40|                        vehicles and equipment. High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park,
  41|                         cemetery and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway-related machinery
  42|                                                                                              64

===PAGE 67=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                         are serviced by one lead mechanic supported by department personnel. Outsourcing of highly
   2|                         technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore more costly. Due to the volatile global
   3|                         market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum-based products continues to be a moving target and
   4|                         therefore difficult to estimate annually.
   5|                                     Our vehicle maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool. Each repair is
   6|                         logged by department and vehicle identification number and memorialized in a data bank. Every
   7|                         vehicle can be referenced for part numbers, required services, as well and repair costs over the life of
   8|                         the equipment. Preventative maintenance continues to be a most valuable component of the program.
   9|                         As Director, I have developed and submitted to Town Officials a vehicle replacement schedule based
  10|                         upon that created by the American Public Works Association in order to more clearly represent our
  11|                         capital needs.
  12|                         FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  13|                         The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DP W:
  14|                                          DPW Facility 520 Chase Road                                   Public Safety Complex
  15|                                           Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                                    Town Hall
  16|                                          Town Beach                                                     Town Common
  17|                                          Ritter Bldg.                                                   Teen Center
  18|                                          Old Primary School                                            Veterans Park
  19|                                           The Eagle House
  20|                                           All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding the school campus.
  21|                          A facilities staff of one individual remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year.
  22|                          Collectively we attempt to keep buildings compliant with current codes to insure a safe, secure work
  23|                          environment. Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of
  24|                          disrupting or disabling necessary services provided to the community. The Facilities Director is a
  25|                         trades. Together with a stable of reliable professionals, we deliver services directly or oversee those
  26|                          functions provided by specialized subcontractors. Unfortunately, the creation of a capital replacement
  27|                          program and a definitive plan for all our buildings has fallen short of expectations. Years of neglect
  28|                          have created a significant backlog of necessary repairs and improvements and the process has become
  29|                          reactionary at best. The Building Reuse Committee reported clearly that asset management relative to
  30|                         our buildings requires a professionally prepared assessment and a long-range capital plan. To this end,
  31|                          our plan for 2021 is to continue with the contracted services of a Project Management Consultant to
  32|                          survey, quantify, project building needs and establish an annual cost. Additionally, we will revisit the
  33|                          Vertex Report and identify "envelope" tasks that if funded, would protect the structures from further
  34|                          exterior failure. Public Works continues its use of a building maintenance software program as a
  35|                         framework for day-to-day activities. Requests for service are acknowledged immediately, prioritized,
  36|                          and completed in a timely manner either in-house or by sub-contracted services. Those individuals
  37|                          requesting service are notified upon completion of the tasks and a history of the repair is filed
  38|                          electronically.
  39|                          Until we as a town can achieve consensus in the form of a Town-wide plan, we will make little
  40|                          progress. For example, years have passed and still there is no clear path as to the future of the old
  41|                          Primary School. As caretakers it is difficult to create a definitive approach given the uncertainty of
  42|                          future use.
  43|                                                                                               65

===PAGE 68=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                          ENVIRONMENTAL
   2|                          Storm Water
   3|                                  As a component of the aforementioned debt exclusion, approximately five hundred thousand
   4|                          dollars of the four million in total has been earmarked for storm drainage including but not limited to
   5|                          repair of the existing collection system, and new construction. We have already exceeded this amount
   6|                          and continue to perform necessary repairs to the town-wide drainage system.
   7|                                     Before roads are paved, sub surface drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed
   8|                          to avoid paving over problems. In the future new Federal and State storm water regulations will likely
   9|                          force the Town to become more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of funding.
  10|                                       At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall, drainage structures in close proximity to
  11|                          open water are cleaned and inspected. Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street
  12|                          sweeping and catch basin cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
  13|                          Waste Water
  14|                                     The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. Day to day maintenance of the
  15|                          overall wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations is outsourced to a specialty vendor but
  16|                          continues to be the responsibility of Public Works. Business management, inspections, and permitting,
  17|                         remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW.
  18|                         Tree Removal
  19|                                      Storm damage together with the natural attrition creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning
  20|                          that far exceeds the allotted budget. The Department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must
  21|                          prioritize work based upon the calculated degree of danger to residents and property. With the
  22|                          establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established
  23|                          through prioritization. Sadly, the amount of work far exceeds the available revenue. Until there is a
  24|                          substantial infusion of funds, the DPW will act in a reactionary mode. All work aside from brush
  25|                          removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket
  26|                          truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff initiates the activity of removing and
  27|                         disposing of woody debris within the right-of-way together with a town-wide program of curbside
  28|                         mowing. An aggressive program by our energy provider in recent years has lessened the burden of the
  29|                          Town to remove those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  30|                         Landfill
  31|                                  The "capped" landfill off Young's Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol
  32|                          mandated by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive
  33|                         methane gas as well as groundwater contamination.
  34|                                     The frequency of the testing, reduced through a special permit as a result of consistently
  35|                          favorable results, remained constant throughout the year. The landfill site including the perimeter
  36|                          fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  37|                                     Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both
  38|                          determined by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are allowed to store these materials through the
  39|                         procurement of a (BUD) Beneticial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of
  40|                         these materials. Other communities must transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at a
  41|                         substantial cost to the community.
  42|                                     On weekends during the fall and spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual
  43|                         composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and
  44|                          our overall recycling program.
  45|                                                                                              66

===PAGE 69=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                     There has to date been no dedicated funding toward the organization or long-range remediation
   2|                          of this collection area. With public water and sewer at close proximity, this acreage has been proposed
   3|                          as a potential recreational area for the Town. As of yet this project has not gained traction.
   4|                          Solid Waste
   5|                                     In the past, Public Works would have the responsibility to monitor, order, store, and distribute
   6|                          trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor,
   7|                          Casella Inc. Pay as you throw... Recently we entered into an agreement allowing our "trash bag"
   8|                          supplier to coordinate the effort between supplier and vendor resulting in some mixed reviews. DPW
   9|                          remains the point of contact between residents and the vendor responsible for curbside pick-up.
  10|                          Lake Shirley Dam
  11|                         In the early 1990s with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding, the Lake Shirley Association turned
  12|                          over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
  13|                          maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed by a small number of volunteers from the
  14|                          Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  15|                          establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the
  16|                          umbrella of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP
  17|                          mandates compliance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and
  18|                          support of a professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection
  19|                          resulted in a positive report and we remain in total compliance.
  20|                          Winter Operations
  21|                          Snow and ice present a major challenge. The safety of the public depends on the ability of the DPW to
  22|                          maintain our roadways at a high level of safety despite the severity or the duration of snow and icing
  23|                          events. To that end, we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan with a
  24|                          flexibility to accommodate the unpredictability of each storm. The expectation of immediate service is
  25|                          high and must be delivered to every part of town in a timely manner.
  26|                                     Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.
  27|                          Financial support is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of
  28|                          services due to breakdowns and repairs. In the fall, salt is delivered to the Chase Rd. facility.
  29|                          Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible
  30|                          price. From late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks,
  31|                          spreaders and plows for winter operations. During the construction season road crews address right-of-
  32|                          way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to eliminate obstacles to snow
  33|                          removal. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations. Typically, DPW staff
  34|                          combines with up to 14 subcontractors to provide service.
  35|                                      Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event, that determines the
  36|                          overall cost. For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a
  37|                          long duration freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the
  38|                          department operates in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter.
  39|                                      It is a dedicated group of individuals who successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer
  40|                          regardless of severity or duration.
  41|                                                             "DW workers are the original Storm Troopers"
  42|                                                                                               67

===PAGE 70=== (ocr, 28 lines)
   1|                            PARKS
   2|                                        In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners, DPW staff oversees and
   3|                            maintains all parks and grounds exclusive of school property. While resources of the DPW are
   4|                            available as required, much of the Town-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, inclusive of
   5|                            the playing fields, resulting in significant savings and increased efficiency over an in-house
   6|                            operation. Marshall Park accommodates the high school baseball and softball schedules, Little
   7|                            League and the Lunenburg Phillies. McNally and Fitzgerald Field provide for an active youth
   8|                            program as well. Commissioners have initiated a fee schedule at the fields and the Town Beach.
   9|                            Personnel are employees of the DPW. Wallis Park Playground is a hugely successful project due
  10|                            primarily to the tireless efforts of the neighborhood group. A state of the art facility now stands
  11|                            proudly in place of a previously run-down, abandoned lot. It is a jewel of the Whalom community.
  12|                            With the recent hire of a Park and Recreation Director, the future is looking bright!
  13|                            CEMETERY
  14|                                        One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North
  15|                            and South cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with
  16|                            the three members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies equipment and support staff
  17|                            for, on average, forty burials a year and general maintenance of the facilities as required by Town
  18|                                               "Every Town Department is, in some manner, and at some time a client of the
  19|                                                                                 Department of Public Works
  20|                                        As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents
  21|                            and fellow Town employees as we persevere.
  22|                                        As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure, our goal is to preserve and enhance the
  23|                            resources of the Town of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed
  24|                            all expectations associated with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public
  25|                            Respectfully Submitted
  26|                            Jack Rodriquenz
  27|                            Director
