===PAGE 71=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                                   FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|                                   (This is a reprint of the message printed in the May 4, 2019 Annual Town Meeting warrant)
   3|                                                                                    Fiscal Year 2020
   4|                        The Town of Lunenburg goes through a budget process that brings together the Town Manager,
   5|                        department heads and citizens to discuss the financial condition of our Town and how to best manage
   6|                        changes in population and funding. In November, the Town Manager provided a target budget to
   7|                        department heads as a guide to develop their budget requests. After individual discussions with the
   8|                        department heads and listening to public comment during the budget presentations, the Town Manager
   9|                        developed a final recommended budget. Hard questions were thoughtfully talked through in pursuit of
  10|                        finding balance between ideal service and what can be funded at projected levels of taxes and new
  11|                        growth. The fiscal year 2020 budget recommendation is for a 3% increase over FY19. The budget
  12|                        would fund targeted additions in staff in response to the increased need for Town services resulting from
  13|                        increased population. It funds the capital plan and the Town's stabilization accounts. It meets all the
  14|                        financial policy requirements as set forth per Town Policy. The budget does not call for increases in
  15|                        debt; the Town would continue to pay down its debt through FY 2020 and beyond. We are pleased to
  16|                        report for the upcoming year Lunenburg remains fiscally healthy.
  17|                               Revenue
  18|                                Lunenburg's total revenues are projected to be $43,133,920. The projection brings in an
  19|                                additional $1,508,020 above what was collected last year. The estimated revenue breakdown
  20|                               for FY20 is:
  21|                                           Source                        Amount            % of Total
  22|                          Tax Levy                   $27,953,184            65%
  23|                          State Aid                  $ 9,250,358            21%
  24|                          Local Receipts             $ 3,010,153              7%
  25|                          Available Fund             $ 2,920,225              7%
  26|                        The tax levy is projected to increase by $904,568: the standard 2.5% increase over last year's tax base,
  27|                        projected new growth of $375,000, and excludable debt. State Aid is projected to increase by 1.9%,
  28|                        although it still has not reached the robust levels seen in past years. The Town benefits from non-
  29|                        municipal revenues (e.g. grants, support groups, and donations). For example, the Library, Council on
  30|                        Aging, and Fire Department have received funding for operating and capital expenses in the past year
  31|                         from sources other than local taxes and state aid, thus keeping those expenses out of the budget. We are
  32|                         grateful for the efforts that all departments put into finding outside sources of funding to help the Town
  33|                         continue to move in a positive direction.
  34|                        Expenses
  35|                         Lunenburg's recommended expenditures are $43,133,920. Out of this total the Omnibus Budget total is
  36|                         $38,058,164, which is an increase of $1,190,260 or a 3.2% increase. Other expenditure categories
  37|                        include Cherry Sheet Charges ($1,215,303), Worcester Regional Retirement ($1,354,353), capital
  38|                        articles ($1,684,100), tax title ($12,000), allowance for abatements ($175,000), funding for stabilization
  39|                        accounts and OPEB ($450,000), and other non-operating expenses ($185,000). The total overall budget
  40|                        increases $1,508,020, or a 3.6% increase.
  41|                                                                                               69

===PAGE 72=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|                           As Lunenburg's population increases, the demand for services increases along with it. To prepare for
   2|                           this the Police Department had developed a ten-year staffing plan, so the increase of one officer this
   3|                           year is the first step. The Fire Department developed a four-year staffing plan to expand to paramedic-
   4|                           level services that is still on track, taking into account the two new hires from last year plus the training
   5|                           and overtime costs budgeted for this year. The Lunenburg Public School budget has been facing
   6|                           upward trends in enrollment. This year the recommended budget funds the school department at a 3%
   7|                          increase. It does not fund the entire amount recommended by the Superintendent. This year's increase
   8|                           follows a 6.7% increase in 2018 and 3% in 2019. Enrollment numbers, school choice in/out ratios, and
   9|                          potential state formula recalculations (particularly related to health costs and special education) will
  10|                           continue to have an impact on school budget recommendations in the future. Described below are
  11|                           expenses that represent a change in Lunenburg's departmental operations or significant line items.
  12|                                     Department                         Purpose                           Increase Amount
  13|                                      General Government                Assessors Software (current       $13,000

  20|                                      Police                            Additional FT Patrol Officer     $80,000
  21|                                      Fire                              Paramedic Training                $21,000

  33|                                                                        Employees
  34|                          The budget includes a capital plan which funds 10 projects recommended by the Capital Planning
  35|                          Committee. Some of the larger initiatives are the Engine 4 replacement ($685,000), Turkey Hill
  36|                          Middle School Asbestos Abatement/Ceiling replacement ($162,000), Fuel Tank replacement at DPW
  37|                          ($220,000), and the Rescue 2 replacement ($310,000). Smaller improvements include painting the
  38|                          Teen Center exterior ($12,000) and the Wallis Park basketball court and fence ($36,000). The entire
  39|                          capital plan for this year, $1,684,100, would be funded in this budget without taking on new debt. Note
  40|                          that three capital improvements totaling $1,119,000, from the Vertex study, related to building repairs
  41|                          for Ritter, TC Passios, and Brooks House are not funded by this budget.. In addition to operating
  42|                          expenses and capital projects the Town is funding line items that serve to provide a cushion for the
  43|                          Town's General Stabilization Fund ($100,000, per Town's Financial Policy) and the Special Purpose
  44|                          Stabilization Fund for future large project capital needs ($200,000), as well as continuing to increase
  45|                          the Other Post-Employment Benefits (OPEB) fund ($150,000.
  46|                          Conclusion - The Town of Lunenburg is in the fortunate position of being able to expand services and
  47|                          improve assets throughout while maintaining financial stability and security. This is reflected in the
  48|                          Town's continuing AA+ bond rating. However, a recent Debt and Liability assessment found that
  49|                          Lunenburg is in a weak debt position. This underscores the importance of not taking on new debt with
  50|                          the Fiscal 2020 budget and will be an important factor when looking ahead.

===PAGE 74=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|                                                                        HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   2|                           The Historical Commission held true to its mission and accomplished much in 2019.
   3|                           The chance find of a gravestone that had been used as a walkway paver led to the Historical
   4|                           Society's research and rediscovery of the Arnold Litch Cemetery near Pleasant Street. The
   5|                          monument was restored and another unused gravestone from the burial plot was repurposed to
   6|                          mount a bronze plaque, bearing testimony to the site and the lives of those there buried
   7|                           Commissioner and professional mason Richard McGrath coordinated with the Historical Society and
   8|                          prepared new granite bases on which to reset the monuments. They have yet to be erected as the
   9|                          search continues for the exact location of the burial plot.
  10|                           The 18th century Battles House at 68 Elm Street was slated for demolition to make way for the
  11|                          development of new homes. The Historical Commission invoked the demolition delay bylaw to seek
  12|                          alternatives. By some combination of pressure from the Commission, cooperation of the property
  13|                          owner, and good fortune, the house was sold intact with its 19th century barn to a new owner who
  14|                          apparently intends to restore or renovate the old house.
  15|                          Several of the road signs marking the Lunenburg Historic District had gone missing over the
  16|                          years and plans were laid to order new signs to be installed by the DPW.
  17|                          The weathervane that once sat atop Town Hall was missing for some time. Commissioner Bill Lakso
  18|                          researched the design of the old weathervane and commissioned a new one to be constructed. The new
  19|                          weathervane had yet to be installed at the close of the year.
  20|                          With some regrets, the Commission approved the total demolition of the historic house at 822 New
  21|                          West Townsend Road. The owner had previously intended to restore the building but after stripping
  22|                          the interior discovered that decay and previous alterations had made the structure worthless.
  23|                          Recognizing the owner's predicament, the Commission approved the demolition application without
  24|                          delay.
  25|                          Lunenburg continues to own historical buildings in the town center. The old Primary School and to a
  26|                          lesser extent, the Brooks House, are falling into disrepair. The Town has no immediate municipal use
  27|                          for these buildings, but voters at Town Meeting have been reluctant to sell them off. Seeing the
  28|                          buildings languish in limbo was a major motivation for Chair Lantry to begin planning for the
  29|                          adoption of the Community Preservation Act, which would generate funds for preservation and other
  30|                          community projects.
  31|                                                                                              72

===PAGE 75=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                        Lunenburg Public Library
   2|                          H. Jackson Brown, Jr., reminds us "that the happiest people are not those getting more, but those
   3|                          giving more." I see evidence of that every day in my colleagues at the library. It is a joy for all of us to
   4|                          work at the library and to see the burgeoning activity here: more than 80,000 items borrowed this past
   5|                          year, almost 70,000 visitors, and more than 8,000 people attending more than 400 programs this past
   6|                          year. Most afternoons you'll find dozens of people doing homework, playing board or video games,
   7|                          researching, using the computers, and even hanging out at the library!
   8|                          I hope you're not surprised that the library is about more than just books. Residents come to the library
   9|                          just to see a smiling face; community members and groups have meetings at the library; families come
  10|                          to story-time; a father and son eat dinner weekly in our cafe for time out of the house; teens and adults
  11|                          volunteer and gain job-like experience; jobseekers come for help on their job search; entire classes of
  12|                          fifth graders come to learn to use the library; people come for help answering a question, to borrow our
  13|                          telescope or electricity use monitor or ukulele; they use ebooks or audiobooks from home or their car;
  14|                          they attend an exercise program or listen to a lecture in the community room; some even watch library
  15|                          programs on the local access cable channel; and of course, many people come to see a movie or
  16|                          borrow and discuss a book.
  17|                          There are new faces on staff at your library. Retirements and graduations mean that five people
  18|                          working at the library are new to their jobs in 2019-we only employ ten total-and the staff is happier
  19|                          than ever to serve our community. The infusion of youth in our staff combines perfectly with several
  20|                          experienced, long-tenured workers with vast amounts of institutional knowledge and dedication. As we
  21|                          move forward into this new era, we are focusing in particular on collaboration and communication. We
  22|                          collaborate amongst ourselves as a staff, we collaborate with other community organizations, and we
  23|                          collaborate with other town departments, all to better serve the community. We also recognize that one
  24|                          of our biggest opportunities is to better communicate to our community all of the services we offer.
  25|                          When someone in town has a challenge, we want them to first ask themselves, "Can the library help
  26|                          me with that?"
  27|                          Our facilities also continue to be renewed. Our building is now more than 15 years old, a fact that
  28|                           surprises many people who enter it for the first time, and to retain that like-new character we must
  29|                          continue to renew it. We have to thank the generosity of the Friends of Lunenburg Public Library for
  30|                          their role in keeping the library in its gem-like condition. They contribute more than $30,000 each year
  31|                          to library goals, including programming, maintenance, collections, and supplies. Without the support
  32|                          of the Friends, the library would be in a much more challenging position.
  33|                           Growing from the successful 2018 renovation of the teen room-one of the key contributing factors
  34|                          that has led to an explosion of teens spending their weekday afternoons in the library-we've focused
  35|                           on creating a space for "tweens" as well. Coupled with a partnership with Turkey Hill Elementary
  36|                           School to bring fifth grade students to the library to learn to use our resources, we've seen an
  37|                           expanding number of library users from the 10-12 year old demographic this past year, too, especially
  38|                          in response to programs targeted specifically at this group.
  39|                                                                                               73

===PAGE 76=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                         Adult Services
   2|                         The biggest highlight for 2019 in adult services has been finding the right person to join the staff to
   3|                         enable a continuation and expansion of recent success. Lydia Gravell, a librarian who grew up in this
   4|                         area, interned and did contract work at Fitchburg State and has a background in Technical Services,
   5|                         was hired in July of 2019, and despite the long search process, the library has come out stronger
   6|                         because she has joined us.
   7|                         That staff addition and our new Library Assistant, Cheryl Stillman, mean that the library has been able
   8|                        to continue to expand the number and variety of programs we offer our adult community. Between
   9|                        July and December 2019 the library's adult services department hosted 29 programs with 460
  10|                        attendees.
  11|                         We continue to experiment with new programming ideas as we seek to keep the library innovative and
  12|                        exciting. One addition, the monthly Craft Lab, has proven to be a great success! In addition, other
  13|                        popular programs included a puzzle swap, music concerts, stitching group meetings, and a chair yoga
  14|                        series. In 2020 the adult services department seeks to continue building on the expectations for the
  15|                        library being known as an educational but entertaining place for everyone to broaden their horizons.
  16|                        Adult Services Goals:
  17|                        • To continue the library's history of providing innovative, educational and entertaining events for
  18|                        adults and people of all ages
  19|                            To improve library partnerships with our community through collaborative programming, and
  20|                        experiment with offsite library sponsored events
  21|                        • To further expand the adult summer reading program
  22|                        • To offer more robust reader's advisory services such as recommendation surveys, read-a-like
  23|                              displays, and book tasting events
  24|                        Children's Services
  25|                        Our community members have already discovered that the Lunenburg Public Library, in addition to
  26|                        being a valued part of our community, is an integral part of early childhood development. Attendance
  27|                        at weekly story times for children ages birth-5 remains strong. Our afterschool offerings for school-
  28|                        aged children include LPL Kids Book Club, which occurs monthly and the children are enjoying the
  29|                        lively book discussions, and related snacks. Enrollment in this group has increased 50% over last year!
  30|                        We also offer monthly Lego Club Challenges that are well attended by both girls and boys and
  31|                        encourages child parent interaction and offers a STEM component.
  32|                        Similarly, our monthly science, technology, engineering, art and math (STEAM) program also targets
  33|                        elementary aged children to encourage and excite these young learners. A strong knowledge base in
  34|                        these core subjects is one of the keys to a successful future, and this focus echoes school curriculum.
  35|                        Our community outreach and support continue to benefit many area schools and groups. In addition to
  36|                        visits from the fifth graders of Turkey Hill Elementary School, we provide weekly story times for an
  37|                        active group of toddlers and families who are part of School Linked Services, preschoolers from the
  38|                        Early Learning Center and various local family day care groups.
  39|                        Thanks to a gift from the endowment of the Friends of Lunenburg Public Library, we now have two
  40|                        early literacy computers with touch screen technology. Featuring games, storytelling programs, and
  41|                        other resources, they are an excellent resource for early learners to develop pre-reading and literacy
  42|                        skills.
  43|                                                                                              74

===PAGE 77=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                        Our annual Summer Reading Program is growing more popular every year. In 2019, we had 312 kids
   2|                        enrolled as active readers who eagerly collected beads as incentives for reading. The beads became
   3|                       necklaces which they proudly wore back to school in the fall to show their new teachers! We had 1,230
   4|                        attendees for the 50 programs that we offered during the summer! We also hosted a summer meals
   5|                        program that supplied nutritional lunches to 322 kids throughout the summer. It doesn't get any busier
   6|                        at the library than summer reading time!
   7|                        Some of us may remember our childhood library as a quiet place with lots of shushing. But today's
   8|                        youngsters have a totally different experience upon entering our library: they engage with books and
   9|                       magazines, with librarians, and with other kids. These opportunities give children a wonderful jumpstart
  10|                        to future literacy and social success!
  11|                        With the vision that every child be familiar and comfortable with the local library and possess the skills
  12|                       to find their way around libraries, the Children's Services goals for 2020 are:
  13|                       • To continue to develop opportunities via passive and active programs designed to encourage
  14|                             children to gain independence and confidence as they explore the many facets of our collection
  15|                       • To provide children with open access to all digital resources and media and to ensure that we have
  16|                              the most up-to-date technology that we can afford for their use
  17|                       • To continually evaluate, increase/update non-fiction collection to present an accurate, updated
  18|                              collection for children of all ages and to optimize shelving of this collection for ease of access
  19|                        Teen Services
  20|                        In FY19, the library offered 190 teen programs which were attended by 1,687 teens. During the summer
  21|                        133 teens read 1,677 hours! Summer program participation ran strong with 255 tweens and teens
  22|                        attending 12 programs. One third of the summer programs were designed with Social Emotional
  23|                        Learning (SEL) objectives in mind. We found success with these programs, and will apply this
  24|                        framework to our future teen programming to improve its effectiveness.
  25|                        2019 saw the introduction of new programs and the expansion of existing traditions. Every summer the
  26|                        library partners with the 4H Worcester Extension Office to provide a babysitting training class for
  27|                        tweens ages 11 and up, and in September, the library responded to a community need with a Babysitting
  28|                        Connection Program so that parents could meet with certified teen sitters to find the best match.
  29|                        This year's new "Volunteen" program is a community service-based program offering teens the
  30|                        opportunity to earn community service hours, engaging in supportive activities, like creating cards for
  31|                        the Pink Revolution Breast Cancer Alliance Initiative and stitching for a cause with the library's
  32|                        Stitchcraft group.
  33|                        The Dungeons and Dragons Club is another highlight, as it continues to grow and now has two distinct
  34|                        campaigns run by different groups. Sharing leadership responsibilities and rotating Dungeon Masters
  35|                        creates learning opportunities and skills-building and allows them to explore storytelling elements.
  36|                        Improvements to the usability of the collection will continue with a diversity audit, evaluating the
  37|                        collection for inclusivity and representation, noting areas for improvement. This will generate data to be
  38|                        utilized in future collection development.
  39|                        Teen Services Goals
  40|                        • Provide an exciting, stimulating fiction collection for children of all ages to encourage a life-long
  41|                              love of reading

   1|                                                         LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   2|                           Mission: Develop, modernize, establish and operate the Town of Lunenburg's Public
   3|                               and Government (P&G) Access Channels to reflect what matters to Lunenburg
   4|                        Publie Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   5|                        Steve Walker, Chair
   6|                        Faith Beall, Vice Chair
   7|                        Jeff Bajko, Secretary
   8|                        Steve Archambault, Member
   9|                         Pete Lincoln, Member
  10|                        The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to
  11|                        manage the Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the
  12|                        town's only cable-service provider.
  13|                         The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
  14|                        broadcast programming from both town functions and meetings, as well as programming from publicly
  15|                         available sources.
  16|                         Programming
  17|                         Channel 9 - Public
  18|                         Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 63% of the local programming on Channel 9 in FY19, up 6%
  19|                         from 57% in FY18. The increase in local programming was largely due to additional videographers.
  20|                         Local programs included high school sports, band concerts and school events. The remaining 37% of
  21|                         programming was obtained through other media sources (Access Share) and online downloads
  22|                        (MassAccess and PEGMedia).

===PAGE 81=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|                        Capital Infusions
   2|                        The renewal of the agreement between Comcast and the Town of Lunenburg commenced on March 1,
   3|                       2018 and runs through February 2028. Part of the finalized agreement was a capital infusion to be
   4|                       broken up over three payments. The second of these payments was received in FY 19 for $42,500.
   5|                       Studio and Equipment Upgrades
   6|                        PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013. We encourage the public to take
   7|                       advantage of the facilities and equipment offered.
   8|                        The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming fiscal years includes acquiring new studio space as
   9|                        soon as municipal space becomes available. A new studio would provide a more permanent and
  10|                        comfortable environment for producing programming.
  11|                       Upgrades in FY19 included the addition of 3 new SONY cameras and tripods for videographers and for
  12|                       loan. A large-scale monitor in the Town Hall Bilotta room was added for better presentation viewing by
  13|                        the audience. New office chairs in the control room and studio were purchased. The Town Hall audio
  14|                        system was adjusted for better performance during meetings with less feedback.
  15|                        The community is encouraged to borrow PAC equipment or submit their own video for Channel 9
  16|                        content.
  17|                        Equipment upgrades accounted for 12% of the FY19 expenses (purchased under Line Item
  18|                        "Equipment/Maintenance and Supplies). "
  19|                        Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  20|                        In FY 19, there was one full-time Manager and 7 part-time videographers, classified as Vendors (Budget
  21|                        Line Item: "Purchase of Services"). The Manager continues to administer the day-to-day operation of
  22|                        the organization, including all production and programming schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and
  23|                        services procurement, studio and inventory administration, advertising, and social media. Vendors
  24|                        (videographers) provided video recording of committee meetings and additional video assignments, as
  25|                        needed.
  26|                         A technical contract consultant works with the PAC manager to provided technical and training
  27|                        assistance as needed. It is expected that he will remain working for PAC through FY20. However, PAC
  28|                        will be searching for a permanent part-time Production Technician to add to the staff by FY21.
  29|                        Of the 63% of locally produced shows on Ch 9, most were produced by public access staff. Our resident
  30|                        top producer, Pete Lincoln of "Talkin' Baseball," continued to create new programs for airing on Ch 9
  31|                        through FY19. Other new programs include Knight's Roundtable and Community News. St. Boniface
  32|                        continued to produce a weekly Mass program, on-air now for over three years.
  33|                        Much effort has gone into recruiting volunteers, with little response. Social media and newspapers have
  34|                         been used to promote volunteerism with little effect. PAC will continue to look for other ways to reach
  35|                        out to the community for volunteers.
  36|                        Staff (Salaries, Health Insurance, Purchase of Services (videographers), Consultant) accounted for
  37|                        79% of the FY19 expenses.

===PAGE 84=== (ocr, 25 lines)
   1|                          Summary
   2|                          FY 19 was a good year for PAC. Local shows increased from FY 18, offering the community more
   3|                          content to enjoy. Additional government meetings were added upon town request providing more
   4|                          informational programming to Channel 8. There was growth in PAC's YouTube channel subscribers,
   5|                          allowing residents to watch programs/meetings at their convenience. The viewership and support of the
   6|                          community as well as PAC's solid commitment to Lunenburg has lead to this success.
   7|                          With the infusion of FY18, FY19 and FY20 capital from Comcast, PAC can now make necessary
   8|                          improvements. It will specifically allow for the addition of a third channel, Educational, to be added in
   9|                          FY20. This channel will focus on School Committee, school sports and events, as well as other
  10|                          imported educational programming. The funding will also help with the increase of administrative and
  11|                         equipment costs.
  12|                          Going forward PAC will continue to focus on content as well as equipment and studio upgrades.
  13|                          A new state-of-the-art studio is essential in terms of providing the public with a media center that is a
  14|                          comfortable place to meet and produce programming. PAC has reserved funding for an eventual new
  15|                          studio, but is waiting for municipal space to become available.
  16|                         In the meantime most local programs are produced outside the studio in "the field," or in venues around
  17|                         town.
  18|                          With the help of several videographers PAC has been able to keep up with recording demand but is in
  19|                         need of volunteers. Volunteers are the "life blood" of media centers around the state and without that
  20|                         involvement PAC will not be able to grow at a desirable rate. Consider volunteering to help provide
  21|                         the necessary resources needed for PAC's continued success. We need you!
  22|                         PAC's promise, as expressed in the mission statement sums it up best: "To Develop, modernize,
  23|                         establish and operate the Town of Lunenburg's Public and Government (P & G) Access Channels to
  24|                        reflect what matters to Lunenburg."
  25|                                                                                              82

===PAGE 85=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                    SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                     CALENDAR YEAR JANUARY 1, 2019 - NOVEMBER 1, 2019
   3|                          The Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a five member committee appointed by the Board of
   4|                          Selectmen.
   5|                          2019 Committee Members:
   6|                          Robert Hamel, Chairperson; Susan Doherty, Vice-Chairperson; Sheila Craigen, Clerk; Nancy Forest,
   7|                          Myleen Mallari
   8|                          The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an
   9|                          opportunity to have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants worked in various
  10|                          town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general office, custodial, library shelving, Meals
  11|                         on Wheels, and kitchen assistance duties.
  12|                          From January 1, 2019 to June 30, 2019 participants received $10 for each hour worked to earn the
  13|                          $1,000 abatement. At this time income eligibility for participants was $40,000 for a one-person
  14|                          household and $60,000 for a two-person household.
  15|                          The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off committee voted at the March 27, 2019 meeting to increase
  16|                          income eligibility for participants of this program to $50,000 for one person households and $75,000
  17|                          for two-person households.
  18|                          Also voted at that meeting was an increase in the hourly wage from $10 to $12 per hour along with
  19|                          an increase to the abatement cap up to $1,500. These increases became effective at the beginning of'
  20|                          FY20 (July 1, 2019).
  21|                          There were seven Lunenburg residents who participated from January 1, 2019 - November 1, 2019.
  22|                          These individuals as a whole, accumulated 837.25 hours which amounted to $8,761.00 in abatements.
  23|                          Eligibility Requirements:
  24|                          * Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  25|                          *Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  26|                          *Provide proof of income by submitting prior year income tax return information
  27|                          * Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name who is also 60 years of age or older
  28|                          *Meet the income guidelines of: one person household - $50,000 / two person household - $75,000
  29|                          *All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re- apply and be
  30|                          accepted annually for participation in the program.
  31|                           Earnings are exempt from state taxes only. Earnings are subject to federal and FICA tax
  32|                          withholdings and are also subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either
  33|                          Worcester Regional Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
  34|                          Departments that participated between January 1, 2019 - November 1, 2012
  35|                          *Lunenburg Board of Health
  36|                           *Lunenburg Building Department/Zoning
  37|                           *Lunenburg Council on Aging
  38|                           *Lunenburg DPW/Sewer Departments
  39|                          *Lunenburg Public Library
  40|                                                                                               83

===PAGE 86=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                                                                        SEWER COMMISSION - FY19
   2|                            The five-member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on
   3|                            May 6, 2006. In May 2018 the Commission re-elected Joe Anderson as Chairman, Carl Luck as
   4|                            Vice-Chairman, and John Reynolds as Clerk. At the May 2019 election, John Reynolds and Carl
   5|                            Luck were re-elected to 3-year terms. On May 28, 2019, the Commission elected John Reynolds as
   6|                            Chairman, Joan Pease as Vice-Chairman and Ryan Stober as Clerk. In early July 2019, Joe Anderson
   7|                            resigned from the Commission as he was moving out of state for his job. The Commission thanks
   8|                            Joe for his service and leadership as Chairman.
   9|                            The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning
  10|                            for the future and developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town's
  11|                            Charter, responsibility for the day-to-day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are
  12|                           under the supervision of the Town Manager who delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain
  13|                           the operational side of the system to the Department of Public Works Director Jack Rodriquenz. The
  14|                           Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 78% of Lunenburg
  15|                           sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 22% is discharged to the City of
  16|                           Fitchburg.
  17|                            The Sewer Commission has contracted with Small Water Systems Services (SWSS) for the
  18|                           operations and management of the ten pumping stations, emergency generators and sewer
  19|                           infrastructure since 2013. In FY19, we completed the 6* year with SWSS as our contractor for the
  20|                           supervision of the town's sewer system. The Commission developed a scope of services for a new
  21|                           O&M contract and put it out for bid in April 2019. Small Water Services (SWSS) won the bid and
  22|                           the Commission began negotiations with SWSS for the new contract effective 7/1/19.
  23|                           There are currently 879 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 352 which have
  24|                           access to Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY19, 24 new accounts connected to Town
  25|                           Sewer. The Commission billed out $835,329.23 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $44,556 of prior
  26|                           year delinquencies added to the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Five properties were
  27|                           assessed a privilege fee in FY19. Two properties assessed were residential. Two assessments were
  28|                           for 24-unit apartment buildings and one other property was a 3-unit condominium building.
  29|                           Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project.
  30|                           Throughout FY19, the Commission continued to work with several different groups regarding their
  31|                           interest in sewer extensions, specifically at Lancaster Avenue, Upland Avenue and Chase Road. The
  32|                           Commission is always willing to discuss a potential sewer extension.
  33|                           In FY 19, the Commission worked on updates to the Grease Trap Regulations, Connection Charges
  34|                           Policy, Irrigation Meter Policy and Single Meter Policy. On October 30 and November 27, 2018,
  35|                           the Sewer Commission held public hearings to inform residents of the proposed changes. The
  36|                           Commission voted to accept the updated policies on November 27, 2019. For the new Irrigation
  37|                           Meter policy, the Commission investigated options for new updated wireless irrigation meters and
  38|                           worked with the Lunenburg Water District to select the new meter which can be read remotely by
  39|                           the Water District on their regular rounds. With the updated Irrigation Meter policy that was
  40|                           effective on August 1, 2019, irrigation meter customers will be charged an annual fee of $50 for
  41|                           the cost to administer the program. The new policy will require residents with deduct meters to
  42|                           convert their unit to one compatible with the Neptune remote reader by July of 2020.
  43|                           The Sewer Commission developed a scope of work for a maintenance plan for the sewer system.
  44|                           The contract was awarded to Wright Pierce and began in FY19. Wright Pierce will conduct an
  45|                           evaluation of the equipment at all pump stations, working with Small Water Systems to develop a
  46|                           written Maintenance Plan to insure the smooth operation of the system. The final plan will be
  47|                           presented in FY20.
  48|                                                                                              84

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                       In FY19, the Sewer Commission developed a scope of work for a Inflow/Infiltration (I/I) 3rd party
   2|                        review, to review the I/I work that has been done and in the past and make recommendations on moving
   3|                        forward in the future. The project was put out to bid and was awarded to Woodward & Curran with
   4|                        work to begin in FY20. The Commission is hopeful that this project will pinpoint the areas that are
   5|                        contributing to excess water entering the system for which the Town must pay Leominster & Fitchburg.
   6|                        The Commission has continued with the annual permit program for the twenty food service
   7|                        establishments with grease traps and interceptors on town sewer. Throughout FY19, the Commission
   8|                        worked with multiple establishments in correcting their non-compliance. The "Rules & Regulations for
   9|                        Oil & Grease Traps/Interceptors for Food Establishments" continues to be a focus of the Commission as
  10|                        Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the
  11|                        sewer system. The Commission developed a FOG Audit inspection checklist and made plans to begin
  12|                        audits of food establishments. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public
  13|                        disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system
  14|                        which increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  15|                        In March 2019, the Commission voted to authorize a contract with Northern Data for bill print
  16|                        outsourcing to streamline the billing process, which has continually grown as more customers have been
  17|                        added to the sewer system. The Commission also voted to join the town's Solar Credit Program in FY19
  18|                        and anticipates saving on energy costs for running the pump stations in FY20.
  19|                        At the May 2019 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward three articles which related to the FY20
  20|                        budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY20, $675,427.86 was appropriated
  21|                        from Sewer Enterprise revenues and the sum of $163,251.14 was appropriated from Retained Earnings
  22|                        for a total appropriation of $ 838,679.00 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY20. The amount of
  23|                        $888, 706.40 was approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service
  24|                        on the sewer betterments or the total approved cost of $1,727,385.40. The second article transferred
  25|                        improve the operating efficiency of the system or cover large emergency expenditures. A main goal of
  26|                        the Sewer Commission continues to be maintaining a balanced budget every year without having to
  27|                        raise rates for our customers. This is the sixth year that the operating budget has been balanced without
  28|                        a rate increase to the users. The third warrant article was to transfer the sum of $656.00 into the OPEB
  29|                        Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles passed at Town Meeting. It was determined in FY 19 that any
  30|                        monies collected for the Sewer Bank (I/I fees) or Reserve Capacity Stabilization Funds would be
  31|                        transferred at the Special Town Meeting following the close of the fiscal year. A portion of the
  32|                        connection charges collected at the time of an application for a permit to connect goes into these
  33|                        accounts and targets resolving I/I issues and future capacity costs paid to the cities of Fitchburg and
  34|                        Leominster for increased capacities.
  35|                        The Commission continued working in FY19 with Adam Costa (Town Counsel of Mead, Talerman
  36|                        and Costa for guidance in contract negotiations and clarification of regulations. The Commission would
  37|                        also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director, for the seamless and smooth operation of the Sewer
  38|                         Division of the DPW. They would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for
  39|                        their partnership and good working relationship with the Sewer Division in providing valuable
  40|                        assistance with meter readings, ownership changes, and status updates.
  41|                           Sewer Commission as of report submittal - February 2020:
  42|                           Carl Luck, Acting Chair Mike Nault, Member
  43|                          Dave MacDonald, Member John Reynolds, Member
  44|                                                                                               85

===PAGE 91=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                                                               BOARD OF HEALTH
   2|                           The Board of Health Office is located in the Ritter building on the corner of Massachusetts Avenue
   3|                           and Lancaster Avenue. Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.
   4|                           The Board of Health, with the assistance of its agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health,
   5|                           continued its mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2019, reviewing
   6|                           Title V Inspection Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing
   7|                           inspections, complaints and general public health related issues as well as resident concerns.
   8|                           The Board of Health oversees the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to moderate income
   9|                           families replace failed septic systems or connect to public sewer. Anyone interested in finding out
  10|                           more about this program can contact the Board of Health office.
  11|                           Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between
  12|                           our agents, other Town departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg
  13|                           but with our partner towns in our region.
  14|                           This year we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new
  15|                           wells, repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with
  16|                           regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its
  17|                           general mandate to protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  18|                           The Board of Health provides other numerous services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the
  19|                           Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinics are
  20|                           provided throughout the year to our residents. These clinics provide inoculations, including flu shots,
  21|                           blood pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and dental screening. Fluoride treatment and dental
  22|                           checks of our youngsters in the School System are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  23|                           The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito
  24|                           Control monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as
  25|                           EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer times of
  26|                           the year. Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:
  27|                           cтmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage at: http://
  28|                           www.cmmcp.org, or calling the Board of Health office. This service is free of charge to residents.
  29|                           With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents recovering
  30|                           from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  31|                           Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  32|                           Please remember that our board members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the
  33|                           citizens of Lunenburg.
  34|                           If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board
  35|                           of Health office at 582-4146 ext. 430.
  36|                                                                                               89

===PAGE 92=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                                                                                         000.
   2|                                                                                         0000     0000°
   3|                                                             NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH
   4|                         Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the local Board of Health in meeting the
   5|                         challenges of environmental and public health needs in Lunenburg. In addition to the day to day public
   6|                         health work conducted for Lunenburg we also provide the following services:
   7|                        • Maintaining Nashoba's internet website to provide information for the public. (See nashoba.org)
   8|                         • Nashoba assisted with providing a flu clinic at the Library to practice emergency preparedness plans.
   9|                        • Response to state mandated changes in regulations requiring more frequent beach sampling through
  10|                               sample collection, submittal for analysis, and follow-up when results were obtained.
  11|                         We look forward to continuing our work with Lunenburg's Board of Health. Included in the day-to-day
  12|                         work of Nashoba in 2019 were the following:
  13|                         • Through membership in the Association, Lunenburg benefited from the services of Nashoba staff
  14|                              including: Registered Sanitarians, Certified Health Officers, Registered Nurses, Nutritionists,
  15|                              Registered Physical & Occupational Therapists, Licensed Social Workers, Certified Home Health
  16|                              Aides, and Registered Dental Hygienists

   6|                                              insure compliance. When a complaint from the public is received an inspection is

   3|                                                                                FIRE DEPARTMENT
   4|                               The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are
   5|                           dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to
   6|                                                                       the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
   7|                                      In 2019 your Fire Department responded to 1885 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   8|                                      Fires & Hazardous Conditions                          420                      Emergency Medical 949
   9|                                      Inspections                                           554                      Other                             26
  10|                                      Current Fire Department staffing is now 7 Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and
  11|                           Emergency Medical Technicians, plus 1 full-time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. The
  12|                           station is manned / days per week with 2 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians, plus a 3r°
  13|                          Firefighter/EMT Monday-Friday from 8 am-4 pm.                                 This coverage is a combination of full-time
  14|                           personnel in conjunction with 88 hours per week of per diem coverage. The per diem coverage is
  15|                          provided by our Call Firefighter/EMTs staffing the station at their hourly rate. Staffing the station with
  16|                           dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs.
  17|                                      Though we have achieved 24 hour coverage in the station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire
  18|                           calls still require more personnel than are on duty in the station. This means we still rely heavily on
  19|                          the dedication and commitment of our Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. Our call
  20|                           personnel remain and will remain for the foreseeable future, a vital part of our emergency response. A
  21|                          new Call Firefighter/EMT must attend over 340 hours of initial training plus additional time in
  22|                          orientation and field training. Members qualifying to drive fire apparatus must complete an additional
  23|                           65 hours of classroom and practical training. On average, it takes a new member one and a half to two
  24|                          years to fully complete this training.
  25|                                      The department continues to move towards the goal of providing our own Paramedic Level
  26|                           Ambulance Service. Though we currently operate two ambulances at the Advanced EMT Level, we
  27|                          must still rely on outside agencies to provide paramedic level care when needed. Firefighter Timothy
  28|                           Downey completed his paramedic training at the end of 2019 and is currently going through the state
  29|                          testing process. Firefighter Austin Flagg also began the training in September and will be completing
  30|                          it at the end of 2020. These two members, plus addition of three more firefighter/paramedics over the
  31|                          next 2-3 years will allow us to provide our own paramedic level care to the community.
  32|                                     In 2019, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 554 Inspections.                                    This is in addition to
  33|                          multiple fire drills and public education initiatives.                     By law we are required to inspect smoke and
  34|                          carbon monoxide detector installations when a home is sold. We are also required to inspect all new
  35|                          oll burner and propane gas installations. Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be
  36|                          inspected for fire safety issues. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans
  37|                          reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process.
  38|                                                                                              94

===PAGE 97=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                         Personnel also continue to provide fire safety education to not only children in our schools but adults as
   2|                         well. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2019 include:
   3|                         → Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire
   4|                              Safety Education in both the schools and with senior citizens. This Grant provides funding for both
   5|                               materials and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.
   6|                              Highlights of activity through this grant include:
   7|                         • Educational programs in basic fire safety in Grades K-3, including use of the Regional Fire Safety
   8|                              Trailer.
   9|                         • Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging. These included cooking and
  10|                               heating safety, a CPR Demonstration and Ice safety.
  11|                        • Training to certify personnel as Public Life Safety Educators. In 2019, Firefighter Austin Flagg
  12|                             joined Captain James Ricci and Lt. Christos Lekaditis as the Department's Life Safety Educators.
  13|                        → Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of
  14|                               walkway sand for senior citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months.
  15|                              In partnership with this program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when carbon monoxide detectors. If the resident was
  16|                               possible, inspects their homes for smoke and
  17|                              lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  18|                         → It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent
  19|                               smoke detectors.
  20|                                    During 2019, Call Firefighters Andrew Bolton and Olivia Hayes graduated from the                   This intensive 4-month
  21|                         Massachusetts Firefighting Academy's Call/Volunteer Training Program.
  22|                         program provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in today's challenging environments. All
  23|                         newly hired Firefighters are required to complete this program within one year of hire.
  24|                                     Seven firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical
  25|                         Rescue and Dive Teams.             These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such
  26|                         as Confined Space,            Rescue from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and
  27|                          Underwater Search and Recovery. In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water
  28|                          Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer.
  29|                                     Apparatus replacement continues to move forward. The new Rescue 2, a 2019 Ford F550/
  30|                          Horton Ambulance was received in late 2019 and is now in service as our primary ambulance. Rescue
  31|                          1 has been rotated to Backup/2n° Ambulance. The replacement for our 31-year-old ladder truck
  32|                         experienced a delay in construction and is now due to arrive in April of 2020. This unit is expected to
  33|                         serve the town for 25+ years. The replacement for Engine 4 has been ordered and is due to arrive in the
  34|                          summer of 2020. This unit is expected to serve the town for 20+ years. The next anticipated major
  35|                         apparatus purchases are not until 2026 (Rescue 1) and 2028 (Squad 3)
  36|                                    In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  37|                          outstanding commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to
  38|                         impress me every day. I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions
  39|                         for their continuing help. Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental cooperation that
  40|                          occurs in the daily course of town business. My thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for
  41|                          their ongoing support of public safety. It is that support that allows us to continue providing the high
  42|                          level of service you have come to expect from us.

   1|                                                                            With Much Appreciation
   2|                                                                               Officer Robert Diconza
   3|                           Bob Diconza began his distinguished career in Law Enforcement as an Auxiliary Police Officer with
   4|                           the Fitchburg Police Department in 1984. He served as a Deputy Sheriff with the Worcester County
   5|                           Sheriff's office from 1987 to 1997 where he served as an Equestrian Officer. Bob was also appointed
   6|                           as a Lunenburg Police Reserve Officer in 1988 during his evolution with the Sheriff's office... In
   7|                           1997 Bob left the police department and Sheriff's office to take a position as a full time law
   8|                           enforcement officer in the City of Myrtle Beach South Carolina. His commission as a reserve officer
   9|                           was reinstated when he returned to Massachusetts in 2002 and in 2004 Bob was appointed as a full
  10|                           time officer. During his time as a full time officer Bob was involved in many aspects of policing
  11|                           serving for many years as the Traffic Enforcement officer. Bob retired from full time policing in
  12|                           2019, but elected to stay on as a Reserve Officer to help with the staffing shortage. We have been
  13|                           extremely honored to work with Bob and grateful that he has elected to stay with us on a part time
  14|                          basis.
  15|                                                            Reserve Officer Stan Barney (Lieutenant Retired)
  16|                           Stan Barney began his distinguished career in Law Enforcement as a Reserve officer with the
  17|                           Lunenburg Police Department in 1975. In 1977 Stan was appointed as a full time officer and attended
  18|                          the Massachusetts State Police Academy. In 1983 Stan left to take a job with the Massachusetts
  19|                          Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals, (MSPCA), as an enforcement officer. During that
  20|                           time Stan stayed on as a Reserve officer and returned as a full time officer in 1996. He was later
  21|                           promoted to Sergeant and then Acting Lieutenant. Stan retired in 2013 but also elected to stay on as a
  22|                          Reserve officer to help with the staffing shortage. In 2019 Stan decided to retire completely giving up
  23|                           his commission as a Reserve officer. We were grateful that Stan stayed with us as long as he did and
  24|                           we have always been extremely honored to serve with Stan. We thank Lieutenant Stan Barney for all
  25|                           his years of exceptional service and wish him the best of luck and the most enjoyment with his
  26|                           retirement.
  27|                                                                           About the Police Department
  28|                           The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. The department is staffed with
  29|                          the Chief of Police supported by the Public Safety Coordinator, and one Administrative Lieutenant.
  30|                          Three Sergeants/Shift commanders, one of the sergeants presently assigned to K-9/Traffic
  31|                          enforcement, eight Patrol Officers, three Student Officers, and eight Reserve Intermittent Patrol
  32|                           Officers make up the patrol unit. Supporting the patrol unit is the Criminal Investigation Unit headed
  33|                           by a Detective Sergeant. Within the C.I.U. is the School Resource Officer who is also assigned to
  34|                           Community Outreach and who is accompanied by Hank the therapy dog. The Lieutenant and Public
  35|                          Safety Coordinator are on duty Monday through Friday between the hours of 7AM and 3PM. Office
  36|                          hours for the Chief of Police are 8AM to 4PM Monday through Friday.
  37|                           The Animal Control Officer responds to domestic animal calls on a 24 hour, 7 day per week basis.
  38|                           This year 356 Animal calls were logged. The public is reminded to report any animal related
  39|                          complaints to the Regional Call Center at (978) 582-4531. For emergencies, dial 911. More
  40|                           information can be found on the Animal Control Web site at lunenburgma.gov.
  41|                           Dogs must be licensed by April 1 annually. Dogs must have a current Rabies certificate to qualify
  42|                           for licensing. This can be done at the Clerk's office in Town Hall, 17 Main Street Lunenburg, MA
  43|                           01462. Unspayed Female/Unaltered Male $20 fee. Spayed Female/Altered Male $5 fee. Late Fee:
  44|                           The front desk is open Monday thru Friday from 7AM to 7PM. Emergency, Non-emergency and
  45|                           911 calls are routed through Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District, (NVRDD).
  46|                                       For all emergencies, dial 911. For general calls, please call (978) 582-4531.
  47|                           Applications for Firearms Licensing can be found on the Police Department Website at
  48|                                                    lunenburgma.gov. Contact (978) 582-4531 for an appointment.

===PAGE 102=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                                     The State of the Police Department
   2|                         The Police responded to 21,816 calls for service this year, a 12.5% increase over last year; responded to
   3|                         295 motor vehicle accidents, investigated 1220 incidents, 22 Field Interviews, conducted radar patrol
   4|                         1840 times, wrote 2580 motor vehicle citations, arrested 161 people, summonsed 319, and placed 8
   5|                         people in protective custody. The K-9 was deployed 27 times the CEMLEC Swat Officer called into
   6|                         service and completed training evolutions on 21 occasions and the Drug Task force was activated 56
   7|                         times. The School Resource officer utilized "Hank," the comfort dog on 612 occasions within the school
   8|                         district for emotional support and many other initiatives such as,                       "read with a dog." Hank was also
   9|                         deployed to a number of funerals for emotional support as well, and to the Domestic Violence Round
  10|                        table, Cub Scout visits and the Senior Center just to name a few. In 2019, staff assignments had to be
  11|                        increased 197 times to effectively meet service demands.
  12|                         The day shift desk officer answered 3056 calls and took 4666 lobby inquiries and complaints in 2019.
  13|                        The day shift desk officer wrote 82 incident reports in 2019. This year the day shift desk watch officer
  14|                        registered 15 sex offenders. The 3 pm to 7 pm desk watch officers assisted in the processing of 55
  15|                        detainees while providing suicide watch and numerous other services including providing support for the
  16|                        patrol staff.
  17|                        Calls for service which will continue to rise, are affected by not only growth in our community, but the
  18|                        surrounding communities as well. Traffic too will continually increase and will have a direct effect on
  19|                        the amount of traffic accidents the police will have to investigate. Environmental factors also play a role
  20|                        in the motor vehicle accident picture. Incidents and arrests amounts are affected by social and economic
  21|                        factors.
  22|                        I again emphasize the important of keeping with current growth trends. Growth invariably plays a major
  23|                        role in police calls for service, arrests, incidents and motor vehicle accidents. The demands for police
  24|                        services will continue to rise and the police department will have to grow in kind.
  25|                        Calls for service have increased by 12.5% over last year. Incidents have increased by 16.7%. Incidents
  26|                        are cases that do not necessarily result in an Arrest, but require a report and investigation. Arrests are
  27|                        up by 33.7%, with motor vehicle accidents decreasing by 12.4%.
  28|                        Group A crimes against Persons has declined by 17% this year. These crimes include; Rape,
  29|                        Statutory Kape, Aggravated Assault, Simple Assault, Intimidation, Kidnapping/Abduction.
  30|                        Group A crimes against Property has also fallen by 17% this year. Crime against Property
  31|                        include; Burglary/Breaking & Entering, Larceny, Shoplifting, motor vehicle theft, Forgery, Fraud, stolen
  32|                        property, destruction of property, vandalism.
  33|                        Group A crimes against Society have increased by 14%. Crimes against Society include; Drug
  34|                        crimes, Pornography/Obscene Material, Weapons law violations.
  35|                        Group B crimes have increased by 22% this year. Group B crimes include; Bad checks,
  36|                        Disorderly conduct, Driving under the influence, Drunkenness, Family nonviolent offenses, Liquor Law
  37|                        Violations, Trespassing, and all other offenses.
  38|                        Lunenburg is a safe community to live in thanks in large part to citizen input. Reporting crime as soon as
  39|                        it's discovered is key to the safety of a Town such as ours. The Police cannot provide effective
  40|                        protection without the citizen involvement. I encourage every citizen to report crime as it occurs,
  41|                        without hesitation, as a timely response is vital to the successfully outcome of any police investigation.
  42|                        "See something, say something."
  43|                                                                                              100

===PAGE 103=== (ocr, 7 lines)
   1|                        Thank you again this year for allowing me the privilege to manage your Police Department. The
   2|                        necessary funding to support my request to bring police staffing to a level that will keep up with the
   3|                       demands of growth has been overwhelming. I ask you to please continue to support our initiatives. The
   4|                        police department endeavors to deliver the service the public expects and is entitled to.
   5|                        Respectfully Submitted
   6|                        Chief James P. Macino
