   1|                                                                                PLANNING BOARD
   2|                          Fourteen (14) ANR plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control
   3|                          Law, but still requiring Board endorsement) were approved.
   4|                          Two large-scale solar fields were approved for construction. 5.8 MW at 994 Northfield Road; this
   5|                          solar field will contain a public foot path easement to allow public access to conservation land on the
   6|                          northerly portion. The other approval was for 2.3 MW at 190 Electric Avenue.
   7|                          Tri Town Landing on Youngs Road has yet to begin construction on Building #5, which will consist of
   8|                          70 units of rental senior housing. Approval was given for nine subdivisions off Howard Street entitled
   9|                          Aro Estates. There will be a total of 122 houses built with construction being phased over a reasonable
  10|                          period of time to avoid overburdening the Town facilities and services all at once. As part of the
  11|                          approval the developer has agreed to donate approximately 40 acres of land to the Town for
  12|                          conservation purposes. Included as part of this donation will be a roadway extension within the
  13|                          subdivisions) to provide public access to the boundary of the Town Forest.
  14|                          Construction continues on Massachusetts Avenue for an office and garage for the Lunenburg Water
  15|                          District. The Board also approved the construction and operation of a retail motor fuel outlet and
  16|                          convenience store with a drive-thru at 400 Electric Avenue.
  17|                          Various businesses asked for, and were approved for waiver requests from the site plan/special permit
  18|                          approval process. One of those businesses was Dick's Market Garden which received approval for
  19|                          construction of a new barn at 554 Lancaster Avenue for indoor washing and prepping vegetables for
  20|                          storage. This process had previously taken place outdoors.
  21|                          Through MRC's District Local Technical Assistance Program a Residential & Commercial Permitting
  22|                          Guidebook was finalized. This Guidebook will facilitate permitting by providing easy-to-follow
  23|                          procedures that outline the permit review process for homeowners, property and business owners,
  24|                          developers, brokers and contractors.
  25|                          Under the Town's Capital Plan, the Town voted to approve $50,000 for FY 2020 for the Board to begin
  26|                          updating the 2002 Master Plan. The Master Plan is a policy document which outlines the community's
  27|                         vision for the future.
  28|                          In spring the Board identified economic development as an important topic to be addressed in the near
  29|                          future. To address this, the Planning Board began working to create an Economic Development
  30|                         Committee (EDC). This group would be composed of a combination of Town Officials, residents and
  31|                         business owners charged with providing advice, and guidance on the state of the business community
  32|                         in Lunenburg and ways that Town Government can support and sustain the local economy.
  33|                         At May 2019 Annual Town Meeting a Zoning Bylaw to regulate Recreational Adult Use Marijuana
  34|                         was passed. This Bylaw allows for no more than two (2) retail locations in the Town of Lunenburg.
  35|                         Retail locations are permitted in the Commercial and Office Park/Industrial Zoning Districts, as long as
  36|                         they are 250 feet away from Residential Zoning Districts and/or residential uses. The remaining types
  37|                         of Marijuana Establishments are permitted ONLY in the Office Park/Industrial District and must be
  38|                         located 500 feet from Residential Zoning Districts and/or residential uses.
  39|                         David Prokowiew stepped down from the Board; Kenneth Chenis finished out his term. Rhonda Lisio
  40|                          and Paula Bertram joined the Board.
  41|                         The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be
  42|                         reached at 978-582-4147 ext. 5. Various forms are available online at https://lunenburgma.gov/on the
  43|                         Planning Board's homepage. The Zoning Map, Code of the Town of Lunenburg, MA (which contains
  44|                         the Protective (Zoning) Bylaws), Rules and Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land, Rules &
  45|                         Regulations for Site Plan Approval & Special Permits, and Permitting Guidebook are available online.
  46|                          The Board continues to work on updating the Zoning portion of the Code.

===PAGE 109=== (ocr, 17 lines)
   1|                           AD HOC OPEN SPACE ADVISORY COMMITTEE TO THE PLANNING BOARD
   2|                         The Open Space Committee's efforts in FY '20 revolved around updating the Town's expired Open
   3|                          Space and Recreation Plan, building on the work done in the prior year. The Plan was drafted in
   4|                          accordance with Massachusetts Executive Office of Energy and Environmental Affairs, Division of
   5|                         Conservation Services' (DCS) guidelines and submitted for their review in January of 2019. The
   6|                          Plan received conditional approval by the State in June of 2019, and the committee focused its
   7|                          energies on completing the required edits to finalize the Plan. The final revised Plan will be
   8|                         submitted to DCS for review and, once approved, will make the town eligible for DCS-administered
   9|                          grant programs through 2026.
  10|                          Working with a diverse group of other boards, the Open Space Committee participated in the Land
  11|                         Acquisition Committee that reviewed, negotiated, and ultimately brought the Saliba property
  12|                         acquisition before Special Town Meeting in November of 2019. The acquisition, which will add
  13|                         approximately 318 acres of land for town conservation and recreation uses, was approved
  14|                         unanimously at town meeting. The Committee continues to support that effort to date.
  15|                         Respectfully submitted,
  16|                          Brandon Kibbe, Chairman

   1|                                                                       ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2|                           The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2019. There were several
   3|                          applicants seeking special permits, special permits for dimensional variations and two applications for
   4|                           variances.
   5|                           The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall
   6|                          at 7:00 PM on an "as needed" basis.
   7|                          The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski,
   8|                          Vice Chairman, Members: Hans Wentrup, David Blatt and Associate member Patrick Callahan. Lisa
   9|                          Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  10|                          We were saddened by the sudden death of our friend and Board member Paul Doherty. Paul served on
  11|                          the Zoning Board.
  12|                          The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  13|                          • To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in
  14|                                the By-laws.
  15|                             To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-
  16|                                laws.
  17|                          • To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts
  18|                                General Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a
  19|                                permit or enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional
  20|                                Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.
  21|                             To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General
  22|                                Laws, Chapter 40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits
  23|                               submitted to the Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.
  24|                                These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances.
  25|                          The office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building at 960 Massachusetts Avenue.
  26|                          Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM, Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday
  27|                          and Thursday, closed Friday.
  28|                         If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals, please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421
  29|                          during regular business hours.
  30|                          Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

   1|                                                              LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS
   2|                                                                   SUPERINTENDENT'S MESSAGE
   3|                                                                             Dr. Kathleen Burnham
   4|                        It is a pleasure for me to present my annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools. This report
   5|                        reflects the progress and good work of newly established teams in the district. It also outlines the
   6|                        strategic objectives that will address district needs and realize the potential for the district I am proud to
   7|                        lead. One of the greatest challenges we face as a community is growing need and limited revenue. I
   8|                        want to acknowledge and express my gratitude for a collaborative approach to projecting and planning
   9|                        for current and future needs with the Town Manager, Heather Lemieux, and all the Town department
  10|                        heads. I appreciate the effort on the part of the School Committee, Board of Selectmen, and Finance
  11|                        Committee to strengthen communication and work collaboratively to problem-solve in order to meet the
  12|                        educational needs of the children of Lunenburg.
  13|                        Because Lunenburg values education, we have two beautiful, modern facilities for teaching and
  14|                        learning; Lunenburg Middle-High School and Lunenburg Primary School. Turkey Hill Elementary
  15|                        School is now fifty years old and we know that the community will have to determine the future of this
  16|                        building in the next few years. A modern facility is but one factor in providing students with a quality
  17|                        education. Rigorous programming designed to meet the academic, behavioral, social and emotional
  18|                        needs of all of our students is foundational to a quality learning experience. It is also essential for the
  19|                        cultivation of a culture of safety, inclusivity, caring, and learning in our schools. At the heart of a
  20|                        quality learning experience is the connection between teacher and student; student-centered teachers
  21|                        who have the knowledge and skills requisite for effective instructional practice; a well-designed
  22|                        curriculum; materials to implement that curriculum; and quality data to assess and monitor student
  23|                        learning. Seamless integration of instructional technology to create engaging lessons, provide
  24|                        differentiated instruction and assessment, and facilitate analysis of achievement data to monitor the
  25|                        growth and support the success of all of our students is also essential.
  26|                        In October 2019, the School Committee approved the Strategy for Continuous Improvement which was
  27|                        developed to address the findings of my entry process last year. The Strategy is designed to meet our
  28|                        Mission:
  29|                        The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence,
  30|                        and passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active
  31|                        citizenship, and rewarding lives.
  32|                        and Vision:
  33|                        The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its
  34|                        values of attending to the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and
  35|                        student-centered instruction for deeper learning at all levels, and promoting the development of
  36|                        attributes and skills necessary for continuous learning for all.
  37|                        The Strategy for Continuous Improvement is a living document which will be reviewed annually and
  38|                        adjusted as needed. The Strategic Objectives for the next three to five years are:
  39|                         Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student-centered and
  40|                        inclusive by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential work tor
  41|                        transforming practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually learning
  42|                        and adapting practice to meet the needs of all students. (DSP Goals: 1, 2, 3, 5, {4, 73)
  43|                        Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous
  44|                        learning in a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values. (District Strategic
  45|                        Plan Goals 1, 6)
  46|                                                                                              111

===PAGE 114=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                         Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication,
   2|                         in order to increase the organization's capacity to execute the district's mission and vision. (District
   3|                        Strategic Plan Goal 8)
   4|                         Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are
   5|                        provided equal access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with
   6|                        an appropriate level of support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient,
   7|                        confident, and capable young adults adequately prepared for future challenges. (District Strategic Plan
   8|                        Goals 1, 6)
   9|                         To cultivate district-wide leadership in key areas identified in the Strategy for Continuous
  10|                        Improvement, the Behavioral Health Advisory Council, Elementary Literacy Team, and Professional
  11|                         Development Committee were created in 2018-2019. In 2019-2020, we added a District Advisory
  12|                        Council and Equity Advisory Council. We expanded the District Leadership Team to include Food
  13|                        Service and Health Service, and created an Instructional Leadership Team focused on improving
  14|                        teaching and learning.
  15|                        In August 2019, the Instructional Leadership Team participated in a data workshop. With guidance
  16|                        from an instructional data consultant, the Team engaged in an analysis of the 2019 MCAS data as a
  17|                        team. We know we must continue to focus on curricular and instructional supports in order to
  18|                        increase the achievement and growth of our High Needs subgroup. Through the analysis, writing
  19|                        emerged as an area for additional focus at all grades. All principals worked with staff to include
  20|                         Student Learning Goals or Professional Practice Goals in writing in the Educator Evaluation Plans.
  21|                         The Team also reviewed the Entry Report of Findings and student survey results and determined that
  22|                         behavioral health (social emotional learning) was another key area in which our educators should
  23|                        focus their Professional Practice Goals.
  24|                                    "One looks back with appreciation to the brilliant teachers, but with gratitude to
  25|                                    those who touched our human feelings. The curriculum is so much necessary raw
  26|                                   material, but warmth is the vital element for the growing plant and for the soul of the
  27|                                   child." ~ Carl Jung
  28|                         As evidence of progress on our objectives, we have been revising and implementing our K-12 social
  29|                        studies and history curriculum to align with the new Massachusetts History and Social Science
  30|                        Curriculum Framework; providing professional development in literacy at Turkey Hill Elementary
  31|                        School and Lunenburg Primary School. Lunenburg Primary School is participating in the second year
  32|                        of a Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) literacy grant. At Lunenburg
  33|                        Primary School we have continued with professional development in behavioral interventions and
  34|                        supports. The LHS staff are engaging in the NEASC accreditation process and have done much
  35|                        curriculum alignment as part of that process. The Behavioral Health Advisory Council and Wellness
  36|                        Committee have conducted student surveys and screenings at LMHS. This data helps us identify
  37|                        general student social and emotional needs and identify students who need intervention or supports.
  38|                        We have made improvements to our before and after school programs in order to provide additional
  39|                        learning experiences.
  40|                        Perhaps the most impactful work of this past year were two professional development conference days
  41|                        created by the Lunenburg Professional Development Committee. The team organized a phenomenal
  42|                        full-day professional development day in September 2019 dedicated to behavioral health. All
  43|                        members of our school community were invited to attend, including teachers, paraprofessionals, food
  44|                        service, secretaries, custodians, and bus drivers.
  45|                                                                                              112

   4|                        High School. The Lunenburg Professional Development Committee is excited to plan a regional

   6|                        The chart below shows the Accountability of the district and schools following the 2019 Spring MCAS.
   7|                        Lunenburg Middle School and Lunenburg High School are performing in the top third percentile. The
   8|                        district needs to dedicate resources K-5 to support behavioral health needs, curriculum alignment,

  28|                                              Intervention
  29|                        Under the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education's accountability system, annual
  30|                        performance determinations for districts and schools are calculated using Indicators: English language
  31|                        arts (ELA), Mathematics, and Science achievement; ELA and Mathematics mean student growth
  32|                        percentile (SP); four-year cohort graduation rate, extended engagement rate, annual dropout rate;
  33|                        student progress towards attaining English language proficiency; chronic absenteeism, and percentage of
  34|                        11th & 12th graders completing advanced coursework
  35|                        Indicators are weighted. Substantial weight for achievement, growth, English language proficiency, and
  36|                        graduation rate indicators is required by federal law. Federal law also requires that these indicators
  37|                        combined should have much greater weight than the additional indicators. Under the accountability
  38|                        system, the weighting of achievement and growth is based on ratios instead of percentages, maintaining
  39|                        the achievement to growth ratio of 3 to 1.
  40|                        Lastly, one of the greatest privileges as Superintendent is to honor a hard-working student with the
  41|                         Superintendent's Award. Every year the selection process is difficult as we have many hard-working,

  43|                        Award is Breda Page-Violette. Breda is the President of National Honor Society and the recipient of the
  44|                                                                                              113

===PAGE 117=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                    Lunenburg Public School Facilities
   2|                                                                                        2019-2020
   3|                           The focus of school maintenance for FY20 continues on preventative maintenance on
   4|                           heating and ventilation systems and energy conservation measures. The focus of repairs has
   5|                           been on Turkey Hill Elementary School with minor improvements such as switch
   6|                           replacement, painting, and other minor items.
   7|                           Security Upgrades
   8|                           The district applied for a Safer Schools and Community Grant in April 2019 and was
   9|                           awarded $55,500 for 32 additional security cameras. The project was bid with Johnson
  10|                           Controls Security Solutions being awarded the work at $45,286.70. Work began in
  11|                           November 2019 and is expected to be complete by January 2020. The number of additional
  12|                           cameras by school was Lunenburg Middle High School, 14, Turkey Hill Elementary School,
  13|                           10, Lunenburg Primary School, 8. Additional memory is being planned for the remainder of
  14|                           the grant funds.
  15|                           Water Testing
  16|                           In the second year of widespread water testing for lead and copper in the drinking water at
  17|                           all schools, in June 2019 lead was detected in a number of sources at Turkey Hill and
  18|                           Passios Elementary School. The source of the lead is believed to be solder from joining
  19|                           copper pipes. A number of high copper readings at Lunenburg Primary School suggest that
  20|                           the water district's corrosion control program needed to be reviewed. The water district has
  21|                           recommended that the ph level of the water needs to be raised. The teaching staff, parents and students have been made aware of the need to flush water sources at the start of the
  22|                           school day. Follow-up testing will occur at all schools in June 2020.
  23|                           Energy Savings
  24|                           The LED lighting conversion for Turkey Hill was completed in April 2019 with $36,233 in
  25|                           Green Community grant funds which paid $14,347.53 for materials and $21,855.47 to
  26|                           Duoba Electrical Contracting to install. The Green Community grant was supplemented by
  27|                           $1719 from the school budget and the remainder of $14,500 from a Unitil Corporation
  28|                           energy rebate.
  29|                           Capital Program
  30|                           The FY20 capital program continued to fund additional asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill
  31|                           Elementary School. In July 2019 Air Quality Experts, for $154,704.29, removed spray-on
  32|                           fireproofing above the ceiling on the south stairway and the second floor hallway of the
  33|                           classroom wing. This project included removal of the splined ceiling and the floor tiles in
  34|                           those areas. Turkey Hill Elementary School is expected to continue to have additional
  35|                           asbestos removal in the coming year with three first floor classrooms still to be abated. A
  36|                            request tor $ 123,000 tor F Y21 was submitted into the town's capital planning process to
  37|                            abate rooms 102, 113 and 114. The school department also requested funds to begin air
  38|                           conditioning design for the Lunenburg Primary School classroom wing and the turkey Fill
  39|                           administration area. Three split air conditioning systems would be added to cover the main
  40|                           office, guidance suite and nurse's office in July 2020 at an estimated cost of $28,000 if
  41|                           approved.
  42|                           Respectfully submitted,
  43|                           John Londa
  44|                           Director of Facilities and Grounds.

   1|                                                                            Technology Department
   2|                                                                                       2019-2020
   3|                         Technology touches all our lives every day at the Lunenburg Public Schools. It is our aim in IT to
   4|                        serve our students, faculty and staff proactively, so that the tools used to facilitate learning function
   5|                        seamlessly and responsively when needed. From checking email, to accessing class information, to
   6|                         connecting and collaborating with students and colleagues near and far, faculty, staff, and IT are
   7|                         working together to help redefine what is possible for our students, so they can redefine what's
   8|                        possible for the world. Our annual report highlights the IT team, as well as our ongoing efforts to
   9|                        improve the technology experience for students, faculty and staff.
  10|                         The IT Team
  11|                         Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and explore
  12|                         innovations in education technology.
  13|                         Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
  14|                               Six years working for the Town and Schools
  15|                               Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and
  16|                                    Belchertown Public Schools
  17|                        Daniel Nadareski - Network Administrator
  18|                        • Retired Police Officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job
  19|                               Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video security
  20|                                    system administration, and IP phone system administration
  21|                         Nicholas Scoggins - School Technology Coordinator
  22|                        • Received Associate and Bachelor of Science degrees from ITT Technical Institute
  23|                              Served 5 years as Security System Administrator at Greater Lawrence Technical School
  24|                        Jordan Pochini - Tech Support Specialist
  25|                        • Graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  26|                              Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail
  27|                                   environments
  28|                        Each year we continue to increase our device-to-student ratio, putting technology directly into the
  29|                        hands of more and more students. This year, we have a ratio of about one device for every 1.4
  30|                        student's districtwide, while just a few years ago that number was closer to one device for every four
  31|                        students.
  32|                        Our ultimate goal remains to provide the tools necessary for faculty, staff, and students alike to
  33|                        facilitate the learning process in a secure, highly-available, high-performing environment, and to
  34|                        always just 'make IT work'.
  35|                        Respectfully Submitted,
  36|                        Steve Malandrinos, IT Director

   1|                                                                          Special Services Department
   2|                                                            FY19 (data taken from 2018-2019 school year)
   3|                                    From the March 1, 2019 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics - District Summary),
   4|                        there are 263 students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last
   5|                        year there were 266). This represents 15.8% of our total student population of 1,662 students it was
   6|                        16.2% last year). The state average from the 2018-19 Selected Populations Report (District) is 18.1%.
   7|                         From the March 1, 2019 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment Statistics - District Summary, the
   8|                         total number of students receiving services outside the district was 12 (last year was 14). Of the 12
   9|                        students, 4 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 5 are in Day Placements and 3 are in Residential
  10|                         Placements. Our Advanced Community Experience Program (ACE), which serves students ages 18-
  11|                        22years old, had 6 students enrolled last year. Additionally, from the March 1, 2019 report, 138
  12|                        students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg (last two
  13|                         years were 118 and 103).
  14|                        This year as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is NOT REQUIRING
  15|                         ASSISTANCE OR INTERVENTION with the Primary School (Insufficient data), Turkey Hill
  16|                         Elementary School (Not requiring assistance or intervention), Lunenburg Middle School (Not requiring
  17|                        assistance or intervention), Lunenburg High School (Not requiring assistance or intervention) and the
  18|                         Advanced Community Experience Program (Insufficient data).

===PAGE 121=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                       Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in
   2|                        Special Education Categories. In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no disproportionality
   3|                        found in either case.
   4|                       Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines. This indicator measures the % of children with parental
   5|                        consent to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is reported for all
   6|                       initial evaluations initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting year. Lunenburg was
   7|                       rated 90% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 100%.
   8|                        Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part
   9|                        C, found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by
  10|                        their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed".
  11|                        Data is suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education
  12|                       services is fewer than 10.
  13|                        Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%.
  14|                        This indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes
  15|                        appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based upon an age
  16|                        appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will reasonably
  17|                        enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's
  18|                        transition services needs. Lunenburg was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in
  19|                        compliance.
  20|                        Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs._ This indicator measures the % of
  21|                        students with IEPs who exited high school during the 2016-2017 school year and self-reported post-
  22|                        school engagement in education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is
  23|                        defined through three measurements:
  24|                        • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  25|                        •   Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving
  26|                              high school.
  27|                        •   Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training
  28|                              program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high school.
  29|                         Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a
  30|                        post-secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2017-2018 school year, state targets
  31|                        for the three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A; 53%; Measurement B: 88%;
  32|                        and Measurement C: 95%. Lunenburg rates                                are as follows (from reporting year 2014-15):
  33|                        Measurement A = B Measurement B= and and Measurement C= 75%.

  19|                                                                    Lunenburg School Lunch Program
  20|                        Lunenburg School Food Service welcomes Zach Welton to our team. Zach is a Lunenburg High alumni,
  21|                        Class of 2019.
  22|                        We started a breakfast pilot in the Middle School on March 25, 2019 and continued through to the end
  23|                        of the school year. This pilot confirmed the need for breakfast in school and we were able to expand
  24|                        breakfast to all of our students at the start of school year 2019-2020. There is a link between breakfast
  25|                       and learning; students experience an improved learning environment. When breakfast is available at
  26|                       school students have improved attendance, decreased tardiness and discipline referrals.
  27|                        Breakfast is available Monday through Friday including early release days. Participation continues to
  28|                        grow. Please check out our webpage located at www.lunenburgschools.net/school-lunchfood-services
  29|                        for details regarding our breakfast and lunch program along with information from the National School
  30|                        Lunch Program. Meal benefits are an ongoing process and you can fill out an application at any time.
  31|                        The federal government requires a new application each school year. Free and Reduced meal
  32|                       applications can be found on our website page listed above.
  33|                        We are happy to have catered the boys' and girls' Varsity and IV soccer banquet again this fall. What a
  34|                        pleasure it is to see the student athletes and their families in this setting. Thank you to Angela Ashton.
  35|                         Dennis Mannon and the Soccer Boosters for supporting our program while celebrating theirs. Check out
  36|                        the catering menu on our webpage and let us cater your next event.

  39|                        Food Service Director

===PAGE 123=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                                                                          Lunenburg Primary School
   2|                        The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students in preschool to
   3|                        second grade. Our students range from three years old to eight years old. Our school community is
   4|                        made up of students, teachers, parents and support staff working together to maintain a learning
   5|                        environment that focuses on the academic and social emotional needs of the whole child.
   6|                        The October 1, 2019 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary
   7|                        Education was 421 students. Currently, our Preschool program services 45 three and four year old
   8|                        students. We have six Kindergarten classes servicing 123 students. Class sizes in our Kindergartens are
   9|                        at 20-21 students. This year we have six first grade classrooms servicing 147 students with class sizes
  10|                        ranging between 23-25. Five second grade classrooms have 119 students with class sizes averaging at
  11|                        24 students.
  12|                        Our professional development has centered on literacy instruction and Social Emotional Learning this
  13|                        year. In early October we received notification from Governor Charles D. Baker that the school was
  14|                        awarded a FY20 Early Grades Literacy grant of $37,705. This grant funded teacher participation in an
  15|                        extensive professional development opportunity that included three statewide literacy institutes and
  16|                        follow-up regional meetings. Along with a consultant who works with teachers to provide clear, specific
  17|                        examples of how the early literacy aligns with our goals and supports the staff in a coaching model.
  18|                        We explored the development of writing in young children (birth-9); the role language plays in literacy;
  19|                        and the content of the Massachusetts ELA/Literacy Framework Standards for Language. Teachers
  20|                        discussed strategies to engage early grades students in writing, and thinking about the content they are
  21|                        learning in order to write with purpose and reason. The grant team shared information with grade level
  22|                        teams throughout the year. Our second grade team has worked with Mary Canner from Teachers21 and
  23|                        they have focused on implementing, Lucy Calkins: Units for Study for teaching reading, this Readers'
  24|                        Workshop model helps teachers provide their students with instruction, opportunities for practice, and
  25|                        concrete doable goals to help them meet and exceed any set of high standards. The main goal of this
  26|                        design is to prepare students for any reading and writing task they will face and to turn kids into life-
  27|                        long, confident readers and writers who display agency and independence.
  28|                        Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. In September
  29|                        and November all of the Primary staff participated in two full days of professional development on
  30|                        Positive Behavior Intervention and Supports (PBIS). Positive Behavioral Interventions and Supports
  31|                        (PBIS) is an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and integrating all of the data,
  32|                        systems, and practices affecting student outcomes every day. The system supports all students through a
  33|                        multi-tiered framework and this year's focus was the Universal Tier (Tier 1). We have worked with
  34|                        Maureen Gilman of Seaside Educational Consultants to develop universal school-wide positive
  35|                        expectations that are practiced throughout our school community. We have worked on establishing and
  36|                        teaching the school wide expectations along with having a continuum of encouraging expected behavior
  37|                        and discouraging problem behaviors. This system is aligning nicely with The Anchors of Emotional
  38|                        Intelligence, a whole-school/district-wide approach to teaching social emotional learning which the staff
  39|                        has been trained in and is using to help students recognize, understand, describe, communicate and
  40|                        regulate their emotions.
  41|                        The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to
  42|                        insure that all students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are
  43|                        completed in the fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing,
  44|                        and math. These common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the
  45|                        progress of students. Struggling students are identified for additional support in Reading, Math, Speech,
  46|                        Occupational and Physical Therapy.
  47|                                                                                              121

   7|                                                                 Turkey Hill Elementary School
   8|                         Our fourth year as Turkey Hill Elementary has gotten off to a strong start. There are currently 364
   9|                        students, with 120 third graders, 124 fourth graders, and 120 fifth graders. The average homeroom size
  10|                        is 24. Our MCAS data from the spring of 2019 indicates we are meeting or exceeding expectations
  11|                         above state averages in the following areas: Grade 3 Math at 55%, and Grade 5 Science at 50%. There
  12|                         are teaching teams of 2 as well as self-contained classrooms at each grade. Special area classes include
  13|                        art, music, PE, library, technology, and computer skills.
  14|                         Teachers are engaging in a variety of trainings. 4* and 5* grade teachers have received professional
  15|                        development in Calkins Units of Study, Reader's Workshop and 3d grade teachers in Writer's
  16|                        Workshop.
  17|                        Math teachers have participated in trainings for IXL math and using Cuisenaire rods for number sense.
  18|                        A full day of professional development in November was dedicated to behavioral health. In December,
  19|                        a number of us visited a charter school in Cambridge with laudatory MCAS scores and others went to
  20|                        Framingham State for a math conference.
  21|                        We are offering many after school activities for students this winter. About 100 students signed up for a
  22|                        variety of goings-on including cooking: they've experimented with graham cracker art and ice-cream in
  23|                        a bag; winter wonderland: sledding and snowman creations on the hill behind LMHS; crafts: sock
  24|                        puppets; science: fake boogers, baking soda and vinegar explosions, and bouncy balls; drama club and
  25|                        chess. We had the Girls on the Run program in the fall with 18 participants, coached by two third grade
  26|                        teachers.
  27|                        Fifth graders spent a week in Beckett, MA enjoying the Nature's Classroom program. The Bridges
  28|                        program introduced students to several senior citizen volunteers who came in to share their life
  29|                        experiences and learn about the younger generation. The six week program culminated in a walking
  30|                        field trip to the Eagle House in December. Grade 5 teachers lead regular walking field trips to the public
  31|                        library every month so students can become good community members with the responsibility of
  32|                        borrowing and returning library books.
  33|                        In June the students attended a Worcester Bravehearts baseball game at Hanover Park and enjoyed the
  34|                        activities for Baseball in Education Day. We finished the year with our first "completion ceremony" for
  35|                        fifth grade students to celebrate the end of their elementary years.
  36|                        The TIGER performances from Plymouth State University provided a program to Grades 3 and 4 in
  37|                        December about how to be a good friend and how to speak up for others. This was underwritten by a
  38|                        generous grant from the Maki Foundation. Fourth graders will welcome the Boston Museum of Science
  39|                        out in the spring to learn about vibrations and how changes in their frequency and amplitude result in
  40|                        different kinds of sound. Third graders took a trip to Monty Tech for a performance of The Nutcracker.
  41|                                                                                              124

===PAGE 127=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                        Turkey Hill Elementary School (cont.)
   2|                        $6100 was again raised during our annual Penny Wars event to support funding for a Chromebook cart
   3|                        and 17 Chromebooks. The PTO generously provided mini grants for teachers and administrators; those
   4|                        funds will be used to purchase a variety of items including a subscription to Brainpop, classroom library
   5|                        titles, sensory tools and a presentation by Rob Surette and his Amazing Hero Art. The Lunenburg Lions
   6|                        Club provided scholarships for Nature's Classroom and regularly provides food donations for students
   7|                        at Extended Day. We appreciate the support from our Box Top collectors and counters, Hannaford
   8|                        Helps supporters, Community Reader day volunteers, parents (both those who support their children at
   9|                        home as well as those who donate hours to school), our Veterans Day and Memorial Day guests and
  10|                        guest speakers.
  11|                        Our Kylee's Kare Kit pilot has expanded to include students from both the Primary school and the
  12|                        middle school. We held a fundraiser in March, a Walk-Jog-athon that raised about $5000 in donations
  13|                        and covers our participation in the program. We provide over 50 students in need weekend bags of
  14|                        food, including winter and spring break and summer vacation. Many staff members from THES and
  15|                        the Primary School volunteer on Friday evenings to prepare over 400 bags for Lunenburg and
  16|                        Leominster students.
  17|                        We continued a band program for grade five students. There are over 30 students who participate and
  18|                        learn their instruments in sectionals and as part of a full band twice weekly. They demonstrate their
  19|                        progress during two yearly performances. All three grades have a general music class once a week and
  20|                            graders are introduced to instruments through recorder instruction. All students also have the
  21|                        opportunity to participate in an after school music program to learn piano, guitar, etc.
  22|                        As an elementary school we understand that our mission extends far beyond the academic. We model,
  23|                        teach and promote the CARES qualities of civility, achievement, respect, effort, and sportsmanship. We
  24|                        provide explicit instruction in coping strategies, self-regulation skills, and calming techniques. New
  25|                        students and staff who join us throughout the year are welcomed on our hallway bulletin board. We use
  26|                        age appropriate picture books at each grade to introduce students to diverse cultures and expand their
  27|                        understanding of the world. We teach responsible digital citizenship in an era where soon they will be
  28|                        inundated with social media. We celebrate the positive role models that exist in our school community
  29|                        with student-of-the-month acknowledgements and Golden Turkey acknowledgements for staff. We
  30|                        encourage children to "Show What They Know" at MCAS time, which demands their best effort. The
  31|                        mission of providing students with the skills, confidence and passion for life-long learning begins
  32|                        during these years, and we collaborate with parents and families to set them on the path to successful
  33|                        careers, active citizenship, and rewarding lives.
  34|                        Respectfully Submitted,
  35|                        Heidi Champagne
  36|                        Principal, Turkey Hill Elementary School

===PAGE 129=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                                                                           Lunenburg Middle School
   2|                        Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that
   3|                        support students' social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-
   4|                        motivated, creative, lifelong learners in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and
   5|                        open honest communication. The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school
   6|                        embraces the philosophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This
   7|                         philosophy is a means by which all students become positive community members that possess the
   8|                        skills to be successful in high school and beyond.
   9|                        Lunenburg Middle School is in its fourth year as a co-located middle-high school serving grades six
  10|                        through eight in a beautiful facility. Effective, student centered middle schools embrace
  11|                        interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable teachers to work with smaller groups of students. Smaller
  12|                        groups of students allow teachers to learn and engage students in their preferred learning style, making
  13|                        them more confident and successful.
  14|                        Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of
  15|                        different interests and talents through special area and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities.
  16|                         One of our goals is for students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue
  17|                        exploring as they move through high school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for
  18|                        students that foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and
  19|                        beyond or what their passion may lead them to.
  20|                        % of Students scoring in the meeting or exceeding expectations range Spring 2018 MCAS
  21|                        In reviewing the results of the 2019 Spring MCAS tests, our percentage of students scoring in the
  22|                                   GRADE                  SUBJECT                  LMS           STATE                DIFF             SGP
  23|                                         6                     ELA                   56%          53%                  +3                60
  24|                                         6                    Math                   65%           52%                +13              58.9
  25|                                        7                      ELA                   47%           48%                 - 1              48.5
  26|                                        7                      Math                   63%           48%               +15               52.6
  27|                                        8                      ELA                     66%          52%               +14               52.5
  28|                                          8                    Math                    64%          46%               +18               53.1
  29|                                        8                Sci and Tech/Eng              69%          48%               + 21              N/A
  30|                         meeting or exceeding expectations range remains strong and above the state average in 6 of the 7 tested
  31|                         areas in the middle school. In 5 of the 7 tested areas of MCAS, Lunenburg Middle school is double digit
  32|                         percentiles above the state average. The Department of Elementary and Secondary Education provides
  33|                         an accountability rating for all schools and districts in the state based upon next generation MCAS
  34|                         testing.

===PAGE 130=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                          Lunenburg Middle School (cont.)
   2|                          Lunenburg Middle School accountability rating for 2019 is designated as not requiring intervention,
   3|                          partially meeting goals. When compared to similar schools in Massachusetts that test similar grade
   4|                          levels, Lunenburg Middle School falls in the 67% percentile, top third in the state. As you can see, our
   5|                          scores in grade eight Science Technology and Engineering are 20% higher than the state average.
   6|                          Although we are proud of our students' performance, we continue to analyze data in our monthly results
   7|                          faculty meetings designed to improve our instruction for all students. Our faculty has identified areas of
   8|                         improvement based upon the data and implemented strategies to improve their instructional practices
   9|                          that will help students succeed
  10|                         We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the needs of each
  11|                         individual student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built into our master schedule to
  12|                          allow teachers time to work with students at their own individual skill level. The philosophy is to
  13|                         challenge students who are high academic achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have
  14|                         identified weaknesses. Teachers are able to work with students in a small group to answer individual
  15|                         questions or provide more individualized support. We design this support model to allow students to
  16|                         feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom. We will continue to refine our intervention
  17|                         block times to best meet the needs of our students. One option for students during this intervention
  18|                         block is the use of our web-based program called IXL. IXL allows students to work at their own skill
  19|                        level, build confidence and moved forward with work that is aligned with the state standards. IXL
  20|                         allows teachers a tool for true differentiation for every student allowing them to work and grow at their
  21|                         own pace and level.
  22|                         Lunenburg Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment classes
  23|                         provide students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of developmental health
  24|                         them to explore their strengths and talents. Students also have the opportunity to sign up for and
  25|                         participate in band and chorus during this block. This enrichment block is in addition to the traditional
  26|                         special area classes students receive, and this block offers students additional physical movement
  27|                         throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on activities and more exposure to the arts,
  28|                        library and technology throughout the school day.
  29|                         We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs
  30|                         developed to promote social and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. We will
  31|                         continue to utilize Developmental Design program to deliver our social emotional curriculum. Students
  32|                         meet with teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem solving, as well as
  33|                         developing empathy and promoting positive self-esteem and strong teacher/student relationships. Our
  34|                         social emotional programs have been developed to allow students to showcase their talents and abilities,
  35|                         grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger generation.
  36|                         We also provide students the opportunity to direct their own learning with a program called Genius
  37|                         Hour. Genius Hour fosters a passion for learning back into the curriculum by having students facilitate
  38|                         their own learning based on their interests.
  39|                         Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that
  40|                         ensure student success and promote a passion for lifelong learning. The key components to our success
  41|                        is our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community
  42|                         who support our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their
  43|                         commitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  44|                                                                                             128

   1|                                                                            Lunenburg High School
   2|                        Lunenburg High School has been busy with academic and social activity in 2019-2020. Teachers have
   3|                        provided our students with rigorous coursework and hold our students to high standards. Our students
   4|                        meet these challenges through collaboration, self-advocacy, and strong work ethic. All students in
   5|                        grades 9-12 have been issued a Chromebook and there are wireless capabilities throughout the building.
   6|                        Numerous teachers are using Google Classroom, the interactive projectors, and voice-lift capabilities.
   7|                        Our students are fortunate to be in a building that can meet the needs of the 21 century learner.
   8|                        During the fall sports season, our students practiced and competed on our fields and artificial turf field.
   9|                        The youth sports teams in the community were scheduled on the fields, as well. During the tall season,
  10|                        most of our fields were being used from 3:00 pm to 8:00 pm.
  11|                        The winter season has been very busy, as well. The gym and weight room are being used for a large
  12|                        portion of the afternoon and evening by our basketball teams, indoor track and field teams, and students
  13|                        and community members in the Adult Education Program. Additionally, we have athletics teams
  14|                        practicing and competing at: Wallace Civic Center, The Groton School, Nashoba Valley Ski Area, and
  15|                        at Fitchburg High School. Our actors and actresses have been rehearsing since January for the
  16|                        Competitive Class Plays.
  17|                        NEASC ACCREDITATION
  18|                        Lunenburg High School is currently going through the accreditation process. Lunenburg High School is
  19|                        currently working on the five priority areas and the graduate profile for the NEASC school growth plan.
  20|                        The five Priority Growth Areas are: Curriculum Maps, Professional Growth/Development, School Civic
  21|                        Engagement, Instructional Practices, and Emergency Response Procedures. The five areas we are
  22|                        currently working on to produce a report for our NEASC accreditation. The final accreditation visit will
  23|                        be conducted in March and will look at how well we are making progress on our growth plan.
  24|                        SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  25|                        Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising and
  26|                        collection endeavors. In the fall, a canned goods drive was held. This was run by NHS and it was a
  27|                        competition with Oakmont to see who could raise the most canned goods for charity. Oakmont won the
  28|                        competition by a narrow margin and both schools helped contribute a lot of non-perishable foods to
  29|                        local food banks. Around the holiday season, advisories, LEA, STUCO, NHS and numerous other
  30|                        agencies collected items for the Lunenburg Gift Card Mart Drive.
  31|                         A Senior English class called community service learning is a class in which students are tasked with
  32|                        researching and identifying needs in the community and then forming an action plan to address the
  33|                        needs. For example, we have students working on: careers that do not require college level degrees.
  34|                        This is to help students make a career choice that does not require a college degree.
  35|                         LHS has held or will be holding presentations for parents/guardians on the following topics:
  36|                        • Planning for High School (grade 8)
  37|                        • Early College Planning (grades 9-10)
  38|                         • Post-Secondary Education Planning (grade 11)
  39|                        • College Application Process (grade 12)
  40|                        • Financing your Education/Financial Aid (grades 8-12)

===PAGE 138=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                          Each year I look forward to the development of the Annual Report, as a means to reflect on the
   2|                          achievements and accomplishments celebrated by students and staff at Monty Tech. The 2018-2019
   3|                          school year was a remarkable one, as our school saw the opening of an all-new training facility,
   4|                          renovated instructional space and state-of-the-art equipment added to three vocational areas, students
   5|                          earning more industry-recognized credentials than ever before, and new partnerships with area colleges
   6|                          and universities. It was a year to remember.
   7|                          Students who attend Monty Tech have chosen a high school experience unlike any other. While
   8|                          completing all of the same high school requirements of students enrolled in a traditional,
   9|                          comprehensive high school, our students are also learning a valuable trade - skills they will carry with
  10|                          them for a lifetime. These vocational skills may provide opportunities for our graduates to work their
  11|                          way through college; they may open the door to new, related career pathways, or they may be the
  12|                          foundation for a lifelong career. The education and training they receive at Monty Tech will open doors
  13|                          and allow them to choose a college and/or career pathway that will define their future.
  14|                          Monty lech academic programs continue to serve students at all levels well. This year, students
  15|                         completed the Next Generation MCAS, and earned commendable passing rates: English Language Arts
  16|                          99%, Mathematics 97%, and Biology 99%. Monty Tech students exceeded the state averages in all 3
  17|                          subjects. In addition, great strides were made in our relatively new Advanced Placement programs,
  18|                          with more than 60% of test takers earning a score of 3 or better. Finally, an all-new science program
  19|                          was fully integrated in FY19 - Biomedical Sciences. This rigorous sequence of courses was met with
  20|                          enthusiasm from students and staff, so plans to expand this Project Lead the Way programming are
  21|                          underway. Our talented academic faculty continue to refine curriculum, incorporate new instructional
  22|                          strategies, and challenge every student to reach new levels, and we couldn't be prouder of the results.
  23|                          And while we are certainly proud of our academic and vocational training programs, a Monty Tech
  24|                          experience would not be complete without participating in meaningful community service. Last year
  25|                          we were honored to once again host the Wreaths Across America Convoy with a remembrance
  26|                          ceremony as it made its way down to Arlington National Cemetery. As the convoy left the school
  27|                          parking lot and hundreds of American flags waved, I was reminded once again of the value of a Monty
  28|                          Tech education. For a moment, our students were taken out of the classrooms and shops and asked to
  29|                          think about what this convoy meant, where it was heading and how they were a small part of the
  30|                          ceremony. They were asked to consider service, and through the waves and cheers it was clear that
  31|                          Monty Tech students knew and understood the meaning behind that experience. It's not often that high
  32|                         school students are asked to stop and think about others and how they might contribute to another
  33|                          person's well-being. But at Monty Tech, our students are not only familiar with the concept, they are
  34|                         committed to community service.                    Whether it is building a carriage house for the NEADS program,
  35|                         designing and building dugouts for area Little League programs, or ensuring handicap accessibility in
  36|                         dated town offices, our staff and students are proud to serve.
  37|                         While students and instructors across the school continue to demonstrate creativity, innovation, and
  38|                         leadership, school leaders continue to keep an eye on emerging trends in vocational education. As we
  39|                          prepare these talented students to make important life choices, we are reminded that in the end, the
  40|                         choice is theirs to make: college or career or both. It is our job to support their aspirations and to
  41|                         educate and train them to achieve at the highest possible level. On behalf of our talented educators and
  42|                         administrators, who remain focused on delivering the highest quality academic and vocational-technical
  43|                         education possible, I am delighted to present the District's 2018-2019 annual report to you, providing a
  44|                         snapshot of the wonderful experiences happening on a day-to-day basis here at Monty Tech.
  45|                         Respectfully submitted,
  46|                             Chile My Marity
  47|                         Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Director

  15|                        Throughout 2018-2019, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents

  17|                        In October 2018, approximately 450 district eighth graders participated in the annual "Tour Day"
  18|                        event. Students toured our twenty-one vocational-technical areas and learned about the school's
  19|                        challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular programs.
  20|                        Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return in the evening with their
  21|                        family members to further explore the facilities and speak with staff members.
  22|                        The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to
  23|                        visit Monty Tech after school and participate in hands
  24|                        on learning experiences across a variety of vocational-technical areas. The program continued to
  25|                        attract a large number of students during the 2018-2019 school year, serving approximately 700 area

  27|                        Class of 2019 Awards
  28|                        Members of the Class of 2019 were awarded approximately $200,000 in scholarships, tools, and
  29|                        equipment. The Monty Tech Foundation generously provided $36,000 in scholarships to graduating
  30|                        seniors, ranging in amounts of $500 to $2,000. The Foundation also awarded $3,250 to the Practical
  31|                         Nursing graduates. Once again, local and state organizations, as well as generous individuals,
  32|                        continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of financial
  33|                        donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates are grateful for this support.
  34|                         Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of
  35|                        higher education. Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a
  36|                        number of articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country.
  37|                        Approximately 38% of the graduating class of 2019 reported plans to enroll at a 4-ear college/
  38|                        university upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will
  39|                        save both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
