   1|                         The final fiscal year 2018-2019 Educational Plan totaled $27,756,374 which represents a 3.2% increase
   2|                         over the 2017-2018 Educational Plan. The District's FY19 budget only exceeds the minimum spending
   3|                         required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by $135,947 or 6%
   4|                         The District was audited in November 2019 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm
   5|                         of Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and has received a very good report.
   6|                         Grants and Contracts
   7|                          Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many
   8|                          educational and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2019, state and federal grant
   9|                         sources provided the school with $945,083. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health
  10|                         Services, Social Intervention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services
  11|                         and Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Marine Corp Junior Reserve Officer Training. The District also received more than $465,000 in competitive grants,
  12|                         including a Skills Capital Grant for $385,000 to purchase equipment for new hospital beds, EKG
  13|                         machines and an Anatomage Table to benefit the Health Occupations and Practical Nursing programs
  14|                         as well as new diagnostic certification tools to benefit the Automotive Technology program. The
  15|                         District    also received       $80,000 in funds to increase school safety.                            Using these allocation and
  16|                         competitive funds, the school was able to purchase a variety of instructional technology, equipment,
  17|                         and supplies to enhance the learning experience.
  18|                         Academic Achievement
  19|                          During the spring of 2019, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued
  20|                         to demonstrate high academic achievement, earning commendable scores on the MCAS in English
  21|                         Language Arts, Mathematics, and Biology. This year, students completed the Next Generation MCAS
  22|                         - a computer-based test that is designed to give students a clear signal on whether they are on track for
  23|                         college and career readiness.              The new standards are more rigorous than the standards for the Legacy
  24|                         MCAS. Monty Tech's passing rate on the English Language Arts was 99%, Mathematics 97%,
  25|                          Biology 99%. Monty Tech students exceeded the state averages in all 3 subjects. With regard to our
  26|                          Accountability status, Monty Tech has made "Substantial Progress Toward Meeting our Targets."
  27|                          Monty Tech is committed to providing students with rigorous STEM learning opportunities, and so in
  28|                         recent years, the curriculum has been expanded to include Advanced Placement Programs (two English
  29|                          courses, two mathematics courses, two science courses, and one computer science course) and teachers
  30|                         have participated in meaningful,                high-quality professional development which has enabled us to
  31|                         successfully implement two Project Lead the Way biomedical courses.
  32|                          The school is in its 7th year of administering Advanced Placement exams and students enrolled in AP
  33|                          courses continue to improve their performance. In the spring of 2019,68 out of 111 students (61%)

  16|                        Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  17|                        Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools, students at Monty Tech are asked to put
  18|                        their education into practice on a daily basis. Students across the twenty-one different vocational-
  19|                        technical education programs are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service
  20|                        vehicles, making walkways more accessible, and performing countless community services.
  21|                         The 2018-2019 school year was a busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide
  22|                         practical, hands- on learning experiences for our students, while helping to improve each of the eighteen
  23|                        member communities.
  24|                        Auto Body Collision Repair Technology:                       The Monty Tech Auto Body shop benefited from new
  25|                        instructional equipment this year. A new silicon bronze MIG welder was added to the program, so that
  26|                        students continue to be trained in the industry's latest technologies. The shop floor was coated with a
  27|                        new epoxy flooring, and a new lift was installed and relocated to replace the old lift, updating and
  28|                        enhancing the instructional space. Three new Vacuum sanders were installed to keep the dust in the
  29|                        shop down, and four new shop benches were installed. In addition to the 80+ community service
  30|                        projects completed last year, students and instructors painted a sign for Hubbardston Council on Aging,
  31|                        as well as one of Monty Tech's vans. All Seniors received their EPA 6 H Certificate, I-Car Pro Level 1
  32|                        in Refinishing and Non- Structural Repair Certification, and a SEM Plastic Repair Certificate.
  33|                        Seniors and two Juniors earned co-op placements, and one Senior traveled to Louisville, KY, competing
  34|                        in the SkillsUSA national competition in June 2018. A 2017 graduate of the program traveled to Russia
  35|                        to compete in the SkillsUSA World competition, where he placed an impressive 14'                                        ". (Total student
  36|                        enrollment: 63)
  37|                        Automotive Technology: As in past years, the Monty Tech Automotive Technology program continues
  38|                       to service a variety of vehicles. Over 400 vehicles were serviced and repaired during the 2018-2019
  39|                        school year, including vehicles brought in by faculty, staff, public, school and students. Students
  40|                        performed repairs that will prepare them for the workplace, such as timing belts, electronic diagnostics,
  41|                       intake manifold gaskets, brake work and various other technical repairs. The program was awarded
  42|                       approximately $200,000 from the FY20 Massachusetts Capital Skills grant program, and will use those
  43|                        funds to provide a more comprehensive electrical and electronics training curriculum. Two Snap-On
  44|                        workforce pipeline for automotive dealerships and businesses.

   1|                        For those students who plan to pursue advanced training, we have updated articulation agreements with
   2|                        3 colleges/universities, as well as the 15 community colleges across Massachusetts. Over the last few
   3|                        years, our students have received more than $28,000 in scholarships to attend University of
   4|                        Northwestern Ohio, and in 2018-2019, a Junior placed 1st in the UNOH nationwide scholarship testing,
   5|                        earning a $5,000 scholarship. (Total student enrollment: 60)
   6|                        Business Technology: Monty Tech's Business Technology program continues to benefit from a unique
   7|                        partnership with Workers Credit Union. The opportunity to participate in teller training and financial
   8|                        literacy workshops has been met with great enthusiasm from students, who completed 9 modules and
   9|                        earned a financial literacy certificate. As a member of the Massachusetts Schools Bank Association
  10|                        through the Federal Reserve Bank of Boston, Monty Tech students are able to compete in the Banking
  11|                        Bowl and Marketing Competitions. Workers Credit Union has also purchased a conference table, 6
  12|                        chairs, and 2 new white boards to support the program. Instructors, in collaboration with their post-
  13|                        secondary colleagues, continue to refine and develop new articulation agreements. These agreements
  14|                        with Mount Wachusett Community College and Keene State College, will provide qualified students
  15|                        with college credits at no cost to the student. Students are eligible to receive 24 credits from Mount
  16|                        Wachusett Community College and 12 credits from Keene State. Opportunities to demonstrate customer
  17|                        service, cash handling, and accounting skills are ever-present as the Business Technology students
  18|                        successfully operate an in-house retail location, The Gear House School Store, and the Monty Tech
  19|                        Greenhouse. Eleven Seniors and four Juniors were out on co-op placements during the 2018-2019
  20|                       school year. (Total student enrollment: 80)
  21|                        Cabinetmaking: Students and instructors in the school's Cabinetmaking program continue to spent time
  22|                        during the 2018-2019 school year manufacturing, assembling and installing maple benches for
  23|                        the Veterinary Science training center. Sophomore students crafted 400 pine beverage caddy's for the
  24|                        2019 Superintendent's Dinner. Using reclaimed maple trees cut from the site of the new science wing,
  25|                        Cabinetmaking students fabricated furniture for Mount Wachusett Community College. Students and
  26|                        instructors also completed projects throughout the school and surrounding communities, to include:
  27|                        work tables and cabinet storage units for Graphic Communications, a large quilt display cabinet and

  29|                        support a recent graduate who now attends WPI, students cut out multiple pieces on the CNC, which
  30|                        will be used in a solar decathlon project in Morocco. A total of 18 students (11 seniors and 7 juniors)
  31|                        earned co-op placements; making this the highest number of students out on Co-op in the program's
  32|                        history. (Total student enrollment: 66)
  33|                        CAD/Drafting & Design: The Monty Tech CAD/Drafting & Design program is increasingly busy each
  34|                        year with outside projects, completing miscellaneous signs and banners for community sports events,
  35|                        craft fairs, and non-profit events. Students also played an instrumental role in 2019 Superintendent's
  36|                        Dinner, designing the layout and décor for this annual fundraising event, and ended the year by
  37|                        designing and installing signage throughout the building for the 2019 graduation exercises. A total of
  38|                        four students (one junior and three seniors) were on Co-op placements, working and learning in area businesses. Four students advanced to the SkillsUSA States competition, representing the program
  39|                        well. With funds awarded from the FY20 Skills Capital Grant, instructors created an all-new
  40|                        instructional space. The new fabrication lab includes equipment to support lessons in 3D printing, 3D
  41|                        scanning, Injection Molding, CNC work and laser cutting. Over the course of the 2018-2019 school
  42|                        year, CAD/Drafting & Design students achieved over 100 industry-recognized certifications in
  43|                        AutoCAD, Inventor, Revit, and Solidworks. 2018-2019 was the most successful year for certifications
  44|                        due to the introduction of SolidProfessor, an online curriculum that was made possible by Perkins grant
  45|                        funding. (Total student enrollment: 58)

  28|                          Radiology exam. All Seniors exposed 2 full mouth series on patients at their externship sites to
  29|                          meet the new requirements for the state. Two Seniors earned co-op placements, while the

  31|                          offices. Students and instructors welcomed Community Health Connections, a school-based dental
  32|                          hygiene program, and provided dental services to more than 60 students in need. Students were
  33|                          given valuable hands-on experience, assisting the staff from CHC during each dental procedure
  34|                          performed. Sophomore students provided dental education to students at Winchendon Memorial
  35|                          Elementary School and Meetinghouse Elementary School in Westminster, while four juniors
  36|                          assisted with the Kid Seal Program at Quinsigamond Community College. A new vacuum pump,

  38|                          feedback devices. (Total student enrollment: 61)

  40|                         Co-operative Education and Student Placement
  41|                         The Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student's technical education that combines
  42|                                           instruction with on-the-job-training. The Co-op Program provides
  43|                         opporunity to further develop acadernic, technisal and employability skills in an industry work
  44|                         environment. All students are eligible to participate in Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and
  45|                         school grade, attendance and performance requirements. All Co-op students have completed the OSHA
  46|                         recognized Career Safe online health and safety course.
  47|                         The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer. For the student, it is a
  48|                         chance to gain on-the-job training that improves both their technical and employability skills.
  49|                         Additionally, making industry connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities. The Co-
  50|                         op Program allows students to earn while they learn.

  25|                         Student Support Services
  26|                         During the 2018-2019 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
  27|                         provided special services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of over two
  28|                        hundred students on Individual Education Plans (TEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to
  29|                        individualized Section 504 plans. While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses
  30|                        special education, the department provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
  31|                        The department includes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated
  32|                         health screening exams, and provides when necessary, health information to the special education team
  33|                        for a student's IEP meeting. The department benefits from a full-time school social worker that
  34|                        participates in departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family
  35|                                   homelessness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing.                                     The school is also
  36|                         fortunate to have on staff a full-time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an
  37|                        initial evaluation or who require a three-year re-evaluation.                         In addition, we have a full-time speech
  38|                        language pathologist, who is available to assist students with disabilities, assess these students and
  39|                         consult with teachers. Our students also have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor
  40|                               part-time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available for scheduled counseling
  41|                         sessions, mental health emergency treatment, medical/mental health re-entry and transition support
  42|                         services, as well as crisis intervention.
  43|                        The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the District's Special Education Program,
  44|                         which is reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive
  45|                        review and evaluation are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the
  46|                         evaluation are used to improve the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett
  47|                        Regional Vocational Technical School.

  16|                        Marine Corps JROTC
  17|                        The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROT) had a productive
  18|                        2018-2019 school year. The Cadet Corps stood a formal Inspector General inspection and achieved a
  19|                        "Mission Ready" grade, and due to outstanding performance throughout the year, Monty Tech's Marine
  20|                        Corps Junior Reserve Officers' Training Corps program was designated as a Marine Corps Honor
  21|                        School. Selection for the Marine Corps Honor School means that the school's Marine Corps JROTC
  22|                        program was in the top 15% of the 68 other programs in Region 1. Region 1 includes schools from the
  23|                        mid-Atlantic and Northeast U.S. The selection is based on several criteria including:
  24|                        • Inspector General's inspection results
  25|                        • Number of cadets in the program
  26|                        • Number of community service hours conducted
  27|                        • Number of public affairs events attended by cadets
  28|                        • Number of academic awards presented to cadets
  29|                        • Participation in drill team competitions, marksmanship competitions, physical fitness competitions,
  30|                              and field trips that support the growth and development of the cadet
  31|                        to each of the three service academies — U.S. Naval Academy, U.S. Military Academy and U.S. Air Because of this distinction, Monty Tech MJROTC instructors are allowed to nominate up to six cadets
  32|                         Force Academy. This is the 8h time the program has earned this distinction since 2007.
  33|                         The Monty Tech JROTC Cadet Corps was honored to once again host the Wreaths Across America
  34|                          Convoy with a remembrance ceremony as it made its way down to Arlington National Cemetery.
  35|                          The Cyber Security Team received national recognition by securing a place to compete in the Air Force
  36|                         Association's National Cyber Security Competition held in Baltimore, MD, for the ninth consecutive
  37|                         year. The competition field began with over 1900 hundred teams battling for just 13 seats, and Monty Tech placed 5* in the highly competitive field in the all service division.
  38|                          The 2018-2019 Corps of Cadets completed over 9,600 hours of community service, conducting a major

  23|                        Practical Nursing Program
  24|                         The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely and
  25|                         ethically in a caring manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in
  26|                         diverse health care settings.
  27|                         On June 27, 2019 a graduating class of 28 students completed the Practical Nursing Program and
  28|                         entered the nursing profession. The class achieved a pass rate of 96%, with 27 of the 28 graduates
  29|                        passing the NCLEX-PN exam (National Council Licensure Examination for Practical Nurses). One
  30|                         graduate will pursue the examination in the coming months.
  31|                         district as Licensed Practical Nurses in various health care settings, such as long-term care, sub-acute
  32|                         care, mental health/ substance abuse facilities, physician's offices and correctional medicine.
  33|                         The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to strengthen the "LPN to BSN (Bachelor of
  34|                         Science in Nursing) Bridge" relationship with Fitchburg State University. Several 2019 graduates are
  35|                         pursuing seats in the LPN to BSN program at FSU and will be continuing their education to the
  36|                         Bachelors in Nursing. Many class applicants cited their attraction to Monty Tech as the opportunity to
  37|                         complete a ten-month accelerated program and then bridge to Fitchburg State University. In the 5-year
  38|                        history of the Bridge Program, all of Monty Tech's Practical Nursing graduates reaching graduation at
  39|                         FSU have passed their RNNCLEX exam.

  17|                          Looking Ahead
  18|                          While the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District educational community is
  19|                         certainly proud of the achievements of our talented students, faculty, and staff, we continue to have an eye
  20|                          toward the future, always committed to improving our vocational and academic programming,
  21|                         strengthening key partnerships,                and maintaining facilities that contribute to student success and
  22|                          achievement. As we look ahead, there are a number of programs and initiatives that we expect will have
  23|                          a positive impact on our school and students for years to come.
  24|                         Expand Health Occupations program to include vital diagnostics: Monty Tech was awarded a generous
  25|                         $385,000 Skills Capital Grant, a portion of which will benefit the staff and students of the Health
  26|                          Occupations program. To better prepare our graduates for a variety of careers in the health sector, the
  27|                          school will purchase new diagnostic equipment to train students in the proper procedures for testing for
  28|                         cholesterol, blood sugar, lead, lung volume, and more. In addition, the program will receive state-of-the
  29|                         -art virtual dissection technology, bringing lessons in anatomy and physiology to new heights. This
  30|                          equipment, provided by Anatomage and most often found on college campuses, is expected to engage
  31|                          students and bring a deeper level of knowledge and understanding to students as they explore the
  32|                          human body and its complex systems.
  33|                         Celebrate the completion of an all-new Automotive Technology simulation lab: The $385,000 Skills
  34|                          Capital Grant award will also fund an all-new Automotive Technology simulation lab, which will
  35|                         include the latest technology in trainers and simulators aligned with NATEF standards. At Monty
  36|                         Tech, students are presented with countless opportunities to earn industry-recognized credentials.
  37|                          These certifications are not only a testament to the skills they have developed while enrolled at Monty
  38|                          Tech, but also will ensure our graduates are more employable upon graduation. To that end, the Monty
  39|                         Tech      Automotive Technology instructors                    have proposed adding NC3 Automotive Diagnostic
  40|                          Certifications to the program.               In partnership with Snap On Tools, Monty Tech will now present
  41|                          students with a more sophisticated technical training program and opportunities to earn all-new
  42|                          diagnostic credentials, validating their experience and ability to diagnose and service today's computer-
  43|                          controlled vehicles.        In addition, the program will also receive new automotive lifts, floor jacks, engine
  44|                         stands, chargers and an air table, to ensure students are trained on equipment that will prepare them for
  45|                         careers in the automotive industry.
  46|                         Roll out affordable veterinary care services in the school's new veterinary clinic: A project more than 7
  47|                         years in the making, the Monty Tech Veterinary Clinic is now open, providing affordable veterinary
  48|                         care to pets across the region. The rollout of services continues throughout the 2019-2020 school year,
  49|                          with routine wellness care visits, affordable medications and vaccinations, diagnostic services, primary
  50|                         care surgical services, stable urgent care, compassionate end of life care, and dentistry.

   4|                                                                       ANNUAL TOWN MEETING
   5|                                                                                   MAY 4, 2019
   6|                        The Moderator called the Annual Town Meeting to order at 9:05 a.m. in the Lunenburg Middle
   7|                        High School auditorium, 1079 Massachusetts Avenue and declared that the warrant was
   8|                        properly served. We pledged allegiance to the flag, the Moderator thanked all elected and
   9|                        appointed officials and recognized Mr. McQuaid who led us in thanking retiring Selectman
  10|                        Jamie Toale for his service to the Town. Robert Bowen named Deputy Assistant Moderator by
  11|                        the Moderator, several announcements were made including ground rules for proceeding with
  12|                        the meeting and at 9:17 a.m. action was taken on Article 1.
  13|                        ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to hear and/or accept the regular written reports of

  16|                        recommendation) Article 1 was PASSED OVER. Already accepted the regular written reports
  17|                        of the Town Officers and Committees.

  11|                         ARTICLE 41. To see whether the Town will vote to adopt a bylaw as tollows:
  12|                         Special Event Permit Guidelines
  13|                        Welcome and Instructions The Town of Lunenburg is proud of the strong sense of
  14|                         community that exists amongst its residents. To further this sense of community the Town is
  15|                        happy to welcome special events from athletic events to concerts to community festivals. The
  16|                         goal of the Town of Lunenburg is to ensure that these events are safe and successful, while
  17|                         minimizing the impact on our residents and businesses. The following instructions are intended
  18|                        to assist applicants in planning and preparing to carry out their special event.
  19|                         Accordingly, the Selectmen shall be authorized to grant permits for special events, as defined
  20|                        in this bylaw, subject to the rules and regulations governing special events set forth herein.
  21|                         Guidelines for Special Events
  22|                         A Special Event is any private event/activity held on public property or an event open to the
  23|                         general public held on private property that involves or affects, directly or incidentally, the
  24|                         ordinary use of parks, playgrounds, fields, public buildings, streets and rights-of-way and/or
  25|                         sidewalks. Special events may include, but are not necessarily limited to, athletic tournaments,
  26|                         concerts, fairs, festivals, holiday celebrations, parades, road or bicycle races, trainings
  27|                         workshops. Properties may hold multiple Special Events, with a maximum of 6 events per
  28|                         private property, in a calendar year given that the appropriate permits, approvals and
  29|                        inspections are in place. Events beyond 6 not permitted without reference to zoning in the
  30|                         district.
  31|                         Individuals or organizations wishing to hold Special Events on public or private property must
  32|                         obtain a Special Event Permit from the Town of Lunenburg. Special Event Permit Applications
  33|                         must be filed a minimum of sixty (60) days prior to the event, failure to do so may result in the
  34|                         denial of application.
  35|                         Exemptions: The following events are exempt from Special Event Permitting:
  36|                         • Town Events held on Town property
  37|                         • Events held in conjunction with the Lunenburg Public Schools or the Lunenburg Public
  38|                               Library
  39|                         • Events held by Private Membership Organizations where only members and guests are
  40|                              invited
  41|                                                                                              173

===PAGE 176=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                         Permit Posting: Special Event Permits should be posted at greeting areas or main
   2|                         entrances at events.
   3|                         Event organizers and managers are reminded that they may be asked by Town staff to show
   4|                         proof of the permit during the event.
   5|                         Timing: Events are prohibited from conducting activities after 22:00 and before 07:00.
   6|                         Loudspeakers, amplified music, bullhorn or public address systems during events are strictly
   7|                         regulated and must be part of the permit.
   8|                         Special Events may span multiple days and include on-site camping (as permitted by the Board
   9|                         of Health) but shall not exceed 4 days. No more than Three (3) multiple day events may be
  10|                         approved per calendar year per private property.
  11|                         Alcohol / drug Use: Alcohol is prohibited in all Town of Lunenburg public property,
  12|                        including buildings, parks, playgrounds, fields, etc. Any event on private property may provide
  13|                        alcohol for a fee, provided that there is strict compliance with the Town of Lunenburg policy
  14|                        regarding such (Town of Lunenburg Board of Selectmen Policies and Procedures, as amended).
  15|                        No Bring Your Own Bottle (BYOB) Permits may be issued for Special Events. Private events
  16|                        that serve alcohol for free or allow participants to provide their own alcohol shall not be subject
  17|                        to this provision.
  18|                        Recreational use of drugs shall be prohibited at any permitted Special Event that takes place on
  19|                         Town of Lunenburg property. This shall be deemed to include marijuana.
  20|                         Restrooms/Trash/Cleanup: The Town of Lunenburg may require event organizers to
  21|                        provide temporary toilet facilities. Temporary toilet facilities must be cleaned daily or as
  22|                        necessary per the Lunenburg Board of Health. Trash must be disposed of in approved
  23|                        containers. Trash pickup and disposal is to be performed as required by the Board of Health.
  24|                        The applicant must clean the right-of-way or public property of all rubbish and debris, returning
  25|                        it to its pre-event condition at the conclusion of the event. If the applicant fails to clean up
  26|                        debris and trash within the specified period, cleanup will be arranged by the Town and charged
  27|                        to the applicant.
  28|                        Traffic & Parking: Parking is permitted in designated areas only. The event organizer
  29|                        will be responsible for providing a parking plan to the Lunenburg Police Department for their
  30|                        approval. Parking may not interfere with event entrances or emergency access.
  31|                        Traffic and Parking Plans shall not violate Chapter 332 of the Code of Lunenburg.
  32|                        Signs: Signs are permitted, though approval may be required by the Building Inspector.
  33|                        No signs may be affixed to trees, buildings or street furniture.
  34|                         Smoking: Smoking is not permitted in any public facility, on school grounds or on
  35|                        recreational parks, fields or playgrounds.
  36|                         Fireworks: fireworks are not permitted within the Town of Lunenburg without proper
  37|                         licensing.
  38|                         Tents: Large Function Tents over 2500 sq. feet require an inspection or permit from the
  39|                        Town of Lunenburg Board of Health, Building and Fire Departments. Such structures require a
  40|                         flame resistant certificate issues by an acceptable testing laboratory and site plan indicating a
  41|                                                                                              174

===PAGE 177=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                        fire lane accessing the tents) or trailers), a fire extinguisher on- site and acknowledgement that
   2|                        no smoking, fireworks, or open flames will be permitted in the tents).
   3|                        Security/Traffic Control: Events may require the hiring of police officers to provide for
   4|                        security and/or traffic control.
   5|                        BUFFER ZONE: All Special Events shall take place no less than one hundred feet (100')
   6|                        from an abutting residential property. Upon submission of a written request supported by
   7|                        documentation the Board of Selectmen may reduce this distance at their discretion under
   8|                        reasonable circumstances.
   9|                        First Aid/Medical: Events may require provisions for first aid and medical personnel.
  10|                        Arrangements should be with a private service for the event. The Fire Department can provide a
  11|                        list of contacts.
  12|                        ACCESSABILITY: Any Special Event permitted through the Town of Lunenburg shall
  13|                        be accessible to all persons who may attend. This includes the provision of accessible parking
  14|                        spaces, paths, restroom tacilities and any other reasonable accommodations that may be
  15|                        required
  16|                        EVENT LAYOUT: All Special Event Applications shall include an Event Layout Plan
  17|                        that includes:
  18|                        Site Access
  19|                        Parking Areas
  20|                        Restroom Facilities
  21|                        Required Buffer Zone
  22|                        Location of any abutting houses
  23|                        Food Service Area (if applicable)
  24|                        Sleeping Accommodations (if available)
  25|                        Wetlands on the property (if applicable)
  26|                        First Aid Station (if applicable)
  27|                        Alcohol Serving Areas (if applicable)
  28|                        Location of Stage or Entertainment (if applicable)
  29|                        Route of Race (if applicable)
  30|                        Any Additional Event Features not listed.
  31|                        Insurance: At the discretion of the Board of Selectmen, all applicants must provide a
  32|                        certificate of insurance. Terms of insurance may be set by the Board of Selectmen in scope and
  33|                        financial amount at their discretion or set at the minimum amount of $1,000,000 for
  34|                        commercial general liability as a precondition for obtaining permits. The Certificate of
  35|                        Insurance must include all coverage deemed necessary for the event, as specified by the Town
  36|                        of Lunenburg and Town Counsel, including indemnification and hold harmless clause. The
  37|                        Certificate of Insurance must name the Town of Lunenburg as an additional insured on
  38|                        applicable policies. This Certificate and verification of Workers Compensation Coverage must
  39|                        be submitted to the Town Manager's Office no later than ten (10) business days prior to the
  40|                        date of the event. Final Special Event Permits will not be issued without submission of a
  41|                        Certificate of Insurance reflecting the necessary Workers Compensation Coverage to comply
  42|                        with State Law.
  43|                                                                                              175

===PAGE 178=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                        Additional Permitting and Cost Requirements: Depending upon the Special Event,
   2|                        additional permits may be required by the Town of Lunenburg Departments. These may
   3|                        include, but are not limited to, permits for temporary food, food preparation, campground,
   4|                         athletic field use, merchandise sales, etc. The organizers of the event are responsible for
   5|                         obtaining any additional permits or licenses as determined by representatives of each Town
   6|                        Department before the issuance of the Special Event Permit.
   7|                        Modification: The Board of Selectmen may at their discretion based on the specifics of the
   8|                        applying event forgo any permit requirement in these guidelines.
   9|                        Enforcement: Town of Lunenburg Police, Fire or other staff so designated by the Town
  10|                        Manager, may request that a resident, event employee, or participant leave any park or public
  11|                        facility for violation of the rules and regulations.
  12|                        Special Event Permit Process:
  13|                        Filing of Special Event Permit Application & Supporting Material with Board of Selectmen
  14|                        Distribution of Material to Relevant Departments for Review
  15|                        Meeting of Relevant Departments to discuss event coordination
  16|                        Submission of Comments from Town Staff to BOS.
  17|                        BOS Public Hearing on Special Event Permit
  18|                        Vote on Special Event and Permit Issuance

===PAGE 184=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                             § 140-1 Purpose.
   2|                              The purpose of this article and associated regulations is to regulate earth removal operations
   3|                              and land filling operations for the protection of human health, public safety, welfare, and
   4|                             the Town's natural resources in the Town of Lunenburg. Further, the provisions of this
   5|                             article are intended to protect abutting property owners from dust, washouts, noise, ponds,
   6|                             and other hazardous land conditions that could potentially be created by poorly managed
   7|                             earth removal and/or filling operations.
   8|                             § 140-2 Definitions.
   9|                             As used in this bylaw, the following terms shall have the meanings indicated:
  10|                             ABUTTERS The owners of property within 300 feet of the perimeter of the subject
  11|                             property.
  12|                             APPLICANT The owner of land from which earth will be removed or fill to be placed,
  13|                             or the person of lawful standing in the stead of such owner. Any person, company, or agent
  14|                             standing instead of the actual property owner must have the owner's written permission to
  15|                             do so.
  16|                             BOARD The Planning Board of the Town of Lunenburg, which shall act as the permit
  17|                             granting authority (PGA).
  18|                             EARTH This term shall include soil, loam, sand, gravel, clay, peat, rock, or other
  19|                             earth material in solid form.
  20|                             EARTH REMOVAL OPERATION Any commercial mining, stripping, quarrying,
  21|                             filling, digging or blasting of earth originating from the Town of Lunenburg and its
  22|                            transportation into or out of the Town of Lunenburg.
  23|                             EROSION The process by which the ground surface is worn by forces such as wind,
  24|                            running water, ice, abrasion, gravity, transportation or by artificial means.
  25|                             FILLING OF LAND The import and use of earth materials on any parcel of land
  26|                             within the Town of Lunenburg.
  27|                             GRAVEL Loose fragments of rock or coarse aggregate resulting from natural
  28|                            disintegration and abrasion of rock or processing of weakly bound conglomerate.
  29|                             LOAM OR TOPSOIL A soil consisting of a friable mixture of varying proportions of
  30|                             clay, sand, silt, and organic matter.
  31|                            PGA The permit granting authority, which will be the Planning Board for the
  32|                             purposes of this article.
  33|                             PROCESSING The sorting or separation of earth materials into distinct categories
  34|                             based on particle size or type usually through the use of a screening process, not including
  35|                             stone crushing.
  36|                                                                                              182

===PAGE 185=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|                             PROPOSED FILL MATERIAL Any geologic, man-made, recycled or processed naterial including in its entirety or as a proportion containing clay, rock, sana, graver, pea
   2|                              ınd sediment. Material meeting the federal and/or state definition of solid or hazardous
   3|                             waste or as toxic, infectious, radioactive, corrosive, or reactive material is specifically
   4|                             excluded.
   5|                             SILT Loose sedimentary material with rock particles usually less than 1/16 mm or
   6|                             less in diameter based on the Wentworth scale of measurement.
   7|                             SITE A distinct portion of one or more contiguous lots, under the same or ditterent
   8|                             ownerships, on which an earth removal and/or fill operation is conducted, or is proposed to
   9|                             be conducted, under the permit.
  10|                             SLOPE An area that is more or less steep, as measured by the vertical rise over the
  11|                              horizontal distance, expressed as a percentage or ratio. For example, a rise of one foot over
  12|                             three horizontal feet is a slope of 33% and is expressed as a ratio of 3:1 slope.
  13|                             STONE CRUSHING The mechanical operation which creates smaller-sized stones or
  14|                             stone products from larger-sized stones, boulders or particles typically using a crushing
  15|                             plant or similar machinery.
  16|                             § 140-3 Applicability.
  17|                             A. All earth removal and earth filling operations in the Town shall provide the following
  18|                             information, in writing, to the Building Inspector within six months of the effective date of
  19|                             this article:
  20|                             (1) The map and parcel number of the subject property;
  21|                             (2) An estimate of the amount of material left to be removed (if earth removal);
  22|                             (3) An estimate of the amount of fill material remaining to be placed (it fill);
  23|                             (4) An estimate of the anticipated annual volume of activity;
  24|                             (5) A description of completed and planned reclamation of the property;
  25|                             (6) The date the operation began; and
  26|                              (7) The anticipated date of completion or cessation of the operations.
  27|                             B. Except as provided in § 140-4 or 140-5, a permit shall be required for any of the
  28|                                   following activities:
  29|                              (1) Earth removal that involves 500 cubic yards or more of material per calendar year
  30|                                   (January through December).
  31|                                                                                              183

===PAGE 186=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|                              (2) The filling of land that involves 500 cubic yards or more of material per calendar year
   2|                                   (January through December), provided that it involves either:
   3|                              (a) The disturbance of two or more acres of land; or
   4|                              (b) The filling of land in excess of 18 inches in depth above the existing grade.
   5|                              C. An earth removal or filling operation that does not exceed any of the above thresholds
   6|                              shall not require a permit but is subject to the following requirements (unless otherwise
   7|                              agreed to, in writing, by the abutting property owner):
   8|                              (1) The operation shall not encroach closer than 10 feet to a property line; and
   9|                             (2) No greater than a 1:1 slope shall exist between the operation and the ten-foot buffer.
  10|                              § 140-4 Exemptions.
  11|                              The following earth removal or fill operations are exempted from the requirement to obtain
  12|                              a permit under this article:
  13|                              A. Earth removal or the placement of fill associated with the installation of septic systems,
  14|                                   which shall be governed by the Commonwealth of Massachusetts Environmental Code
  15|                                   (Title 5, 310 CMR 15.000).
  16|                             B. Earth removal or the placement of fill associated with the installation of foundations
  17|                             for new buildings and/or building additions, which shall be governed by MGL c. 143 and
  18|                             the Commonwealth of Massachusetts Building Code (780 CMR).
  19|                             C. Earth removal or the placement of fill proposed for land falling within the Town's
  20|                              Floodplain Protection District, which shall be governed by §§ 250-4.8 of the Lunenburg
  21|                             Zoning Bylaw and require a special permit from the Planning Board.
  22|                             D. Earth removal or the placement of fill proposed for land falling within the Town's
  23|                             Wetland Protection Bylaw and associated buffer zones, which shall be governed by the
  24|                              Wetlands Chapter within the Town's General Bylaws (Chapter 239), which is administered
  25|                             by the Conservation Commission, and the Massachusetts Wetlands Protection Act.
  26|                              E. Earth removal or the placement of fill that involves less than 500 cubic yards.
  27|                             F. Earth removal or the placement of fill, where the operation occurs entirely within an
  28|                                   individual parcel and where a Town-accepted public way is not used for the
  29|                                   transportation of the material.
  30|                             G. Earth removal or the placement of fill within the right-of-way for a new subdivision
  31|                             road that has been approved by the Planning Board or Zoning Board where there is already
  32|                             a bond in place with the Town to ensure performance.
  33|                                                                                              184

===PAGE 187=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                            The aforementioned items that are exempted provided that they don't exceed any of the
   2|                            following thresholds:
   3|                            i. the earth removal and/or fill operation is limited to no more than three contiguous lots and
   4|                            does not exceed a total area of six acres, and
   5|                            ii. the operation is not conducted, maintained, and/or left in a condition so as to alter the
   6|                            natural drainage flow beyond the property; or to cause dust, silt, soil, or other materials to be
   7|                            deposited on adjacent properties; or to otherwise cause nuisances, hazards, or other
   8|                            objectionable conditions detrimental to health, safety, or property values in adjacent areas.
   9|                            ili. the excavation is not active for a period of time greater than is reasonable for the activity
  10|                            being conducted.
  11|                            iv. Any earth removal or fill is limited to the necessary scope of the project.
  12|                            The PGA shall, upon petition by an abutter or by any Town Official or Town Board, review
  13|                            an operation that would be exempt from the permit requirement pursuant to this § 140-4 and
  14|                            may determine that, because of the nature and scope of the earth removal or fill operation, a
  15|                            permit is nonetheless required.
  16|                            § 140-5 Continuation of Existing soil removal operations.
  17|                            A. An existing operation that had been issued a permit by the Board of Selectmen under
  18|                            the Earth Removal Bylaw that was in effect prior to the effective date of this Bylaw may
  19|                            continue to operate according to the terms of such permit, which shall hereatter be
  20|                            enforceable by the PGA pursuant to this article.
  21|                            B. An existing earth removal and/or fill operation that was not required to obtain a permit
  22|                            under the prior Earth Removal Bylaw, but which exceeds the thresholds specified in § 140-
  23|                            3B of this article, may continue to operate without being required to apply for or obtain a
  24|                            permit for a period of three years from the effective date of this article, provided that the
  25|                            owner or operator of said operation submits to the Building Inspector the information
  26|                            required in § 140-3A within six months of the effective date of this article.
  27|                            C. The right of an existing operation to operate pursuant to § 140-5A under a permit
  28|                            previously issued by the Board of Selectmen, or to operate without a permit pursuant to
  29|                            § 140-5B, shall expire if the operation is discontinued. An operation shall be deemed to have
  30|                            been discontinued if no earth removal or fill activity occurs at the site for a period of 12
  31|                            consecutive months.
  32|                            § 140-6 Permit criteria for earth removal and fill operations.
  33|                            A. Permits for earth removal and/or fill operations shall be granted by the PGA only upon
  34|                            its written determination that the proposed use shall not cause substantial detriment to the
  35|                            neighborhood, or the Town, taking into account the characteristics of the site and the
  36|                            proposal in relation to the site and surrounding environment.
  37|                                                                                              185

===PAGE 188=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                              In addition to any specific factors that may be set forth elsewhere in this article and its
   2|                              associated regulations adopted pursuant to § 140-8, such determination shall include
   3|                              consideration of each of the following:
   4|                          (1)      Social, economic, or community needs which are served by the proposal;
   5|                         (2)       Traffic flow and safety, including loading and unloading;
   6|                         (3)        Neighborhood character;
   7|                          (4) Impacts on the natural environment; and
   8|                         (5) Potential fiscal impact, including impact on Town services, tax base and property
   9|                                values.
  10|                         B. Determination for each of the five criteria shall be set forth in the permit decision as findings
  11|                         of fact.
  12|                         § 140-7 General limitations.
  13|                         A. No permit shall be issued for the removal of earth or the placement of fill in any location if
  14|                         such an operation:
  15|                         (1) Will endanger the public safety, public health or constitute a nuisance.
  16|                        (2) Will produce noise, dust, or other noxious effects observable at the lot lines of the property
  17|                        in amounts objectionable or detrimental to the normal use of adjacent properties.
  18|                         (3) Will result in the transportation of materials in such manner as to cause traffic congestion,
  19|                         dust, spillage, noise, or other nuisances or hazards, particularly on residential streets.
  20|                        (4) Will result in the transportation over ways which will be unduly injured thereby.
  21|                        (5) Will result in a change of topography and cover which will be disadvantageous to the most
  22|                         appropriate final use of the land or to the use of lands adjacent to the site.
  23|                        (6) Will cause irreparable harm to or loss of important wildlife, wildlife habitat or rare plant
  24|                        species indigenous to the area.
  25|                        (7) Will result in stormwater damaging abutting properties.
  26|                         B. Applicants are encouraged to submit a written statement regarding how each of the previous
  27|                        items will be addressed and/or mitigated.
  28|                         § 140-8 Regulations.
  29|                         The PGA may adopt and periodically amend rules and regulations for the implementation of
  30|                        this article, by majority vote. Such rules and regulations may set forth performance standards
  31|                        for earth removal and fill operations, impose filing and consultant fees, define additional terms
  32|                        not inconsistent with the article, and establish administrative procedures. Failure by the PGA to
  33|                        adopt such rules and regulations or a legal declaration of their invalidity by a court of law shall
  34|                        not act to suspend or invalidate the effect of this article.
  35|                         § 140-9 Permit procedures.
  36|                             A. Any person wishing to obtain a permit to remove from or place earth material on a
  37|                             property in the Town, or to use any public way within the Town for transporting such
  38|                             material, shall file a completed application for a permit together with any required
  39|                                                                                              186

===PAGE 189=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                             supporting data and maps with the PGA and the filing fee, as established in the regulations
   2|                             of the PGA.
   3|                             B. Any permit issued is nontransferable and shall automatically expire upon completion of
   4|                             the earth removal or fill project for which it was issued or at such time as may be specified
   5|                              in said permit. In no case shall a permit be issued initially for a period longer than three
   6|                             years. A permit may be renewed for up to two years or lesser time as determined by the
   7|                              PGA after evidence is presented that all conditions of the expiring permit have been
   8|                             complied with and the work has been performed in good faith. There is no limit on the
   9|                             number of permit renewals an applicant can apply for. A public hearing may be required by
  10|                              the PGA, at its discretion, for renewal of permits.
  11|                              § 140-10 Public hearing.
  12|                             A. Once a complete permit application is filed with the PGA, the PGA shall set a date for a
  13|                             public hearing and so notify the applicant. Notice of the hearing shall be published by the
  14|                             PGA, at the applicant's expense, in a newspaper of general circulation in each of two
  15|                              successive weeks, the first publication being not less than 14 days before the day of said
  16|                             hearing. Notice of the hearing shall be given to all owners of real estate abutting upon the
  17|                              land specified in the permit application or lying within 300 feet of the subject parcel, all as
  18|                              appearing on the most recent tax list. The PGA will arrange for the publication and
  19|                              transmission of the notice of the hearing to the abutters, and the costs will be billed to the
  20|                             applicant. Final approval for the permit shall not be made until all hearing fees have been
  21|                              paid in full.
  22|                               B. The PGA's final action, rendered in writing, shall consist of either:
  23|                                     1) Approval of the site plan based upon determination that the proposed plan will
  24|                                           constitute a suitable development and is in compliance with the standards set forth
  25|                                          in this Bylaw; or
  26|                                     2) Disapproval of the site plan based upon a determination that the proposed plan does
  27|                                     not meet the standards set forth in this Bylaw; or
  28|                                     3) Approval of the site plan subject to any condition, modification or restriction
  29|                                     required by the Planning Board which will ensure that the project meets the standards
  30|                                     set forth in this Bylaw.
  31|                              § 140-11 Bond or secunty.
  32|                               The Board may require suitable bond or other security adequate to assure compliance with
  33|                              any of the provisions of this bylaw.
  34|                              § 140-12 Enforcement; violations and penalties.
  35|                              A. The PGA or its authorized agent shall enforce this article, its regulations, and the
  36|                              requirements and conditions of permits issued thereunder and may pursue civil and criminal
  37|                              remedies for violations of the same.
  38|                              B. The Building Inspector is authorized to conduct inspections on behalt of the PGA. To
  39|                              the extent permitted by law, or if authorized by the owner, operator, or other person in
  40|                              control of the site, the Building Inspector or other agent of the PGA may enter on the site to
  41|                               conduct inspections.
  42|                                                                                              187

===PAGE 190=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                             C. The Building Inspector or other authorized agent of the PGA may issue such orders as
   2|                             are deemed necessary to stop violations and ensure compliance with this article, its
   3|                             regulations, and permits, including an order to cease and desist operations. Such orders may
   4|                             be issued by certitied mall, return receipt requested, or by delivery, to the property owner,
   5|                             permit holder, or person responsible for operations at the site.
   6|                             D. The PGA may seek injunctive relief to restrain violations or to compel abatement or
   7|                             remediation of violations.
   8|                             E. Any person who violates any provision of this article or regulations, permits, and orders
   9|                            issued thereunder may be punished by a fine of not more than $300 per offense. Each day
  10|                             that such violation occurs or continues shall constitute a separate offense.
  11|                             *. As an alternative to a criminal fine, the Town may utilize the noncriminal disposition
  12|                             procedures of MGL c. 40, § 2ID, in which case the Building Inspector shall be the enforcing
  13|                            person. The penalty for the first offense shall be $100. The penalty for the second offense
  14|                            shall be $200. The penalty for the third and subsequent offenses shall be $300.
  15|                             G. If the violator holds a permit issued under this article, the PGA may revoke the permit
  16|                            after a public hearing, at which point all operations shall cease until such time as the
  17|                             necessary measures are taken to assure compliance with this article and a new permit is
  18|                            issued.
  19|                            § 140-13 Severability.
  20|                            The provisions of this article are severable, and the invalidity of any section, subdivision,
  21|                            paragraph, or other part of this article shall not affect the validity or effectiveness of the
  22|                            remainder of the article.
